*   **Thorough Ablation Study:** The comprehensive ablation study helps provide clear evidence of the contribution of each module in the FoundationalECGNet.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Public Datasets:** While the use of publicly available datasets promotes reproducibility, it could also limit the model's generalizability to diverse clinical populations. A more thorough analysis involving diverse datasets would strengthen the claims.
*   **Limited Novelty in Individual Components:**  While the *integration* of the different components is a valuable contribution, each component itself (wavelet denoising, GAT, TST, CBAM) has been used in prior works, not only in general but also in ECG analysis. The key is the novel combination and its application.
*   **Lack of comparative discussion:** The paper states the performance compared to SOTA models but doesn't compare it to other foundational models apart from stating the number of parameters.
*   **Limited Specificity:** The paper could benefit from a more in-depth exploration of specificity, especially in the context of disease classification. The model has high accuracy but it isn't clear if this means there are many false positives when classifying an already abnormal ECG.
*   **Rule-based disease labelling:** The use of static thresholds based on clinical findings can lack precision. The neuro-symbolic methodology is interesting, but the static nature of the symbolic component isn't ideal.

**Significance and Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in the *integrated architecture* and its focus on creating a lightweight, interpretable, and generalizable solution for automated ECG analysis. The combination of techniques is not entirely novel, but the specific configuration and application for multi-task cardiac analysis are significant. FoundationalECGNet contributes by bridging the gap between highly specialized models and clinically deployable solutions. It moves towards a more practical approach suitable for a wide range of clinical settings. The effort in creating a model suitable to multiple tasks is commendable.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a significant, rather than groundbreaking, advance. While it leverages known components, it creates a novel architecture specifically engineered for ECG analysis with an emphasis on practical considerations (lightweight, interpretable, generalizable). The results show state-of-the-art results and it can be a base for future works.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Global Constraint LLM Agents for Text-to-Model Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08970v1)
- **Authors**: Junyang Cai, Serdar Kadioglu, Bistra Dilkina
- **Abstract**: Natural language descriptions of optimization or satisfaction problems are challenging to translate into correct MiniZinc models, as this process demands both logical reasoning and constraint programming expertise. We introduce a framework that addresses this challenge with an agentic approach: multiple specialized large language model (LLM) agents decompose the modeling task by global constraint type. Each agent is dedicated to detecting and generating code for a specific class of global constraint, while a final assembler agent integrates these constraint snippets into a complete MiniZinc model. By dividing the problem into smaller, well-defined sub-tasks, each LLM handles a simpler reasoning challenge, potentially reducing overall complexity. We conduct initial experiments with several LLMs and show better performance against baselines such as one-shot prompting and chain-of-thought prompting. Finally, we outline a comprehensive roadmap for future work, highlighting potential enhancements and directions for improvement.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel agentic framework for translating natural language descriptions of optimization problems into MiniZinc models. The framework decomposes the complex modeling task by assigning specialized Large Language Model (LLM) agents to detect and generate code for specific types of global constraints. A final assembler agent then integrates these constraint snippets into a complete MiniZinc model. This approach aims to simplify the reasoning challenge for each LLM by focusing on smaller, well-defined sub-tasks. Initial experiments show improved performance compared to one-shot and chain-of-thought prompting baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using specialized agents, each focusing on a specific type of global constraint, is a significant contribution to NL4Opt. While multi-agent frameworks are emerging in the field, this paper's constraint-centric agent design provides a distinct advantage by further reducing each agent's cognitive load and potentially improving accuracy in constraint identification and modeling. This specialization hasn't been explored in depth within the natural language to constraint programming literature, making it reasonably novel.

*   **Significance:** The task of translating natural language to formal models (like MiniZinc) is crucial for democratizing optimization and constraint programming. By improving the automation of this process, the paper has the potential to make these powerful tools accessible to a broader audience. The initial results demonstrate a clear improvement over existing prompting methods, signifying a step towards more reliable and usable NL4Opt systems. The proposed roadmap for future work further enhances the potential impact by outlining avenues for refinement and scalability.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-defined problem decomposition.
    *   Specialized agents reduce the complexity of the reasoning task.
    *   Initial experimental results show promising improvements over baselines.
    *   The paper proposes a comprehensive roadmap for future research and development.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental evaluation is limited in scope. It relies on a relatively small dataset and only includes open-source LLMs, which may not reflect the performance of state-of-the-art models.
    *   The approach relies heavily on prompt engineering, and the details of the prompt design are not fully elaborated.
    *   The assembler agent seems to carry a significant burden of ensuring completeness and correctness, and its performance might be a bottleneck. More detail on the assembler and how it handles conflicts between the agent outputs needs to be provided.
    *   The paper lacks a thorough error analysis. Understanding the types of errors made by each agent and the assembler is crucial for guiding future improvements.
    *   The evaluation focuses primarily on detection rates, leaving a gap in terms of solution quality. Evaluating on more difficult problems would show the full power of the idea.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in NL4Opt by highlighting the benefits of specialized agent architectures. It could also inspire the development of more modular and interpretable NL4Opt systems. The focus on global constraints is a practical approach, and the proposed roadmap provides a valuable direction for future work. The work can serve as the base for more work to improve these systems and use constraint programming more widely.

* **Revised evaluation**: Given the detailed breakdown above, I would like to revise my score for this submission. The primary weakness is the limited evaluation and the fact that it is a very nascent work with many future directions. Given all of that, I think the novelty and significance warrant a score of 'Score: 6'. The framework itself has merit, but the actual improvements may be easily offset by other approaches that could give similar results. It will be important to see in the future whether the agentic approach provides a significant improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### When FinTech Meets Privacy: Securing Financial LLMs with Differential Private Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08995v1)
- **Authors**: Sichen Zhu, Hoyeung Leung, Xiaoyi Wang, Jia Wei, Honghui Xu
- **Abstract**: The integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) into financial technology (FinTech) has revolutionized the analysis and processing of complex financial data, driving advancements in real-time decision-making and analytics. With the growing trend of deploying AI models on edge devices for financial applications, ensuring the privacy of sensitive financial data has become a significant challenge. To address this, we propose DPFinLLM, a privacy-enhanced, lightweight LLM specifically designed for on-device financial applications. DPFinLLM combines a robust differential privacy mechanism with a streamlined architecture inspired by state-of-the-art models, enabling secure and efficient processing of financial data. This proposed DPFinLLM can not only safeguard user data from privacy breaches but also ensure high performance across diverse financial tasks. Extensive experiments on multiple financial sentiment datasets validate the effectiveness of DPFinLLM, demonstrating its ability to achieve performance comparable to fully fine-tuned models, even under strict privacy constraints.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DPFinLLM, a novel privacy-preserving, lightweight Large Language Model (LLM) specifically designed for on-device financial applications. It addresses the growing concern of sensitive data leakage during the fine-tuning of LLMs. DPFinLLM combines differential privacy techniques with a streamlined architecture (inspired by Llama2 and ChatGLM2) to balance computational efficiency, privacy protection, and task performance on edge devices. The model is fine-tuned using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) and a privacy-enhanced training pipeline. Experimental results on multiple financial sentiment datasets demonstrate that DPFinLLM achieves performance comparable to fully fine-tuned models, even under strict privacy constraints. The paper also explores zero-shot performance on unseen datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant Problem:** The paper addresses a crucial and timely problem: securing financial data privacy in the context of on-device LLMs. As financial applications move to edge devices, data privacy becomes paramount, and the paper directly targets this challenge.
    *   **Novelty:** The combination of a lightweight LLM architecture with differential privacy, specifically tailored for on-device financial tasks, appears novel. The DPFinLLM model and training methodology are clearly presented.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments are well-designed and use multiple financial sentiment datasets for evaluation. The paper investigates different privacy constraint levels (epsilon values) and evaluates both fine-tuned and zero-shot performance. Comparisons to relevant baselines (Llama2, ChatGLM2, and FinGPT variants) provide context.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The research has practical implications for developing secure and efficient on-device financial applications.
    *   **Open Source:** Makes the code and experimental details available on Github

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While the paper compares to baselines, it lacks detailed ablation studies to isolate the impact of each component of DPFinLLM (e.g., the lightweight architecture vs. the DP mechanism). This would strengthen the argument that both contribute significantly to the overall performance.
    *   **Limited Scope of Datasets:** While the use of multiple datasets is a strength, they are all focused on sentiment analysis. The paper would be more convincing if the proposed framework was also evaluated across a range of financial tasks like fraud detection, risk management, or credit scoring.
    *   **Zero-Shot Generalization:** The zero-shot performance evaluation reveals some challenges in generalization. More analysis of the reasons for poor zero-shot performance on the FIQA dataset when fine-tuned on TFNS could have been provided.
    *   **Privacy-Accuracy Tradeoff:** While the paper shows that DPFinLLM can achieve comparable performance to fully fine-tuned models under privacy constraints, it does not thoroughly discuss or analyze the precise privacy-accuracy tradeoff in different scenarios. A more detailed analysis of this tradeoff is essential when deploying privacy-preserving techniques.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Privacy Analysis**: While it applies the (ε, δ)-DP mechanism, the paper omits the theoretical privacy analysis, especially in terms of Composable Gaussian Mechanism based on Privacy Amplification by Subsampling, which requires the calculation of privacy loss when composing multiple calls.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper contributes to the field by providing a practical approach to secure on-device financial LLMs. The combination of a privacy-preserving technique with a lightweight LLM architecture makes it relevant to the growing trend of edge AI. The research could inspire further work on privacy-preserving techniques for LLMs in resource-constrained environments.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:**

The paper addresses an important problem with a novel solution and provides solid experimental evidence. The weaknesses related to the lack of ablations, scope of datasets, and deeper analysis of zero-shot performance prevent it from achieving a higher score. Also, the limitation in terms of missing theoretical analysis on the privacy aspect is important to note. While the experimental results are promising, the paper could be strengthened with more detailed analysis and a broader range of evaluations. The paper has moderate significance, but not as outstanding as a score of 8 or more. The work is clearly a contribution to the area, but the limitations prevent it from being considered a ground-breaking advancement. It is a good, solid contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### YouthSafe: A Youth-Centric Safety Benchmark and Safeguard Model for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08997v1)
- **Authors**: Yaman Yu, Yiren Liu, Jacky Zhang, Yun Huang, Yang Wang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used by teenagers and young adults in everyday life, ranging from emotional support and creative expression to educational assistance. However, their unique vulnerabilities and risk profiles remain under-examined in current safety benchmarks and moderation systems, leaving this population disproportionately exposed to harm. In this work, we present Youth AI Risk (YAIR), the first benchmark dataset designed to evaluate and improve the safety of youth LLM interactions. YAIR consists of 12,449 annotated conversation snippets spanning 78 fine grained risk types, grounded in a taxonomy of youth specific harms such as grooming, boundary violation, identity confusion, and emotional overreliance. We systematically evaluate widely adopted moderation models on YAIR and find that existing approaches substantially underperform in detecting youth centered risks, often missing contextually subtle yet developmentally harmful interactions. To address these gaps, we introduce YouthSafe, a real-time risk detection model optimized for youth GenAI contexts. YouthSafe significantly outperforms prior systems across multiple metrics on risk detection and classification, offering a concrete step toward safer and more developmentally appropriate AI interactions for young users.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper "YouthSafe: A Youth-Centric Safety Benchmark and Safeguard Model for Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces YAIR (Youth AI Risk), a novel benchmark dataset designed to evaluate and improve the safety of Large Language Model (LLM) interactions with youth (ages 13-21). YAIR consists of 12,449 annotated conversation snippets covering 78 fine-grained risk types specific to youth, such as grooming, emotional overreliance, and boundary violations. The authors systematically evaluate existing LLM moderation models on YAIR and find that they underperform in detecting youth-centered risks. To address this, they propose YouthSafe, a real-time risk detection model optimized for youth-GenAI contexts, which significantly outperforms prior systems. The paper contributes a novel dataset, a comprehensive evaluation of existing safeguards, and a new model tailored to youth-specific risks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in its youth-centric focus. Existing safety benchmarks for LLMs often concentrate on general-purpose risks like toxicity and model misuse, neglecting the unique developmental and psychological vulnerabilities of younger users. YAIR fills this gap by providing a fine-grained taxonomy of youth-specific harms and a dataset specifically designed to assess these risks. The development of YouthSafe, while building on existing architectures, is novel in its specific optimization for the YAIR dataset and the youth-GenAI interaction context.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work stems from the increasing prevalence of LLMs in the lives of teenagers and young adults. By highlighting the limitations of current safety benchmarks and providing a tailored solution, the paper addresses a critical need for safer and more developmentally appropriate AI interactions for young users.  The YAIR dataset has the potential to catalyze further research in this area by providing a valuable resource for researchers and developers. YouthSafe offers a concrete step toward mitigating the risks associated with youth-GenAI interactions.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Taxonomy:** The detailed three-tier risk taxonomy is well-researched, drawing from developmental psychology and youth online safety research.
*   **High-Quality Dataset:** The YAIR dataset combines real-world and synthetic data, ensuring both realism and coverage of diverse scenarios. The annotation process is rigorous, with measures taken to ensure inter-rater reliability and annotator welfare.
*   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of existing commercial and open-source safeguard models, revealing their limitations in the youth context.
*   **Effective Model:** YouthSafe demonstrates significant performance gains over prior systems, showcasing the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Coverage of Synthetic Data:** The synthetic dataset covers only 78 out of 91 low-level risk types due to LLM refusal to generate certain scenarios.
*   **Snippet-Based Evaluation:** The evaluation is snippet-based, which may overlook risks that emerge over multiple conversation turns.
*   **Self-Selection Bias:** The chat log dataset may be subject to self-selection bias, as it was collected from youth who voluntarily submitted their conversations.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of AI safety by raising awareness of youth-specific risks and providing a concrete benchmark and model for addressing these risks. The YAIR dataset could become a standard resource for evaluating LLM safety in youth contexts, driving the development of more effective safeguards. YouthSafe offers a practical solution that can be adopted by developers of youth-facing AI applications.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a gap in the existing literature and providing practical solutions for improving the safety of LLM interactions with youth. While the limitations are noteworthy, they do not significantly detract from the overall significance of the work. The paper's youth-centric approach, comprehensive dataset, and effective model represent a substantial advancement in the field.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 8/10

### Fast attention mechanisms: a tale of parallelism
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09001v1)
- **Authors**: Jingwen Liu, Hantao Yu, Clayton Sanford, Alexandr Andoni, Daniel Hsu
- **Abstract**: Transformers have the representational capacity to simulate Massively Parallel Computation (MPC) algorithms, but they suffer from quadratic time complexity, which severely limits their scalability. We introduce an efficient attention mechanism called Approximate Nearest Neighbor Attention (ANNA) with sub-quadratic time complexity. We prove that ANNA-transformers (1) retain the expressive power previously established for standard attention in terms of matching the capabilities of MPC algorithms, and (2) can solve key reasoning tasks such as Match2 and $k$-hop with near-optimal depth. Using the MPC framework, we further prove that constant-depth ANNA-transformers can simulate constant-depth low-rank transformers, thereby providing a unified way to reason about a broad class of efficient attention approximations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Fast attention mechanisms: a tale of parallelism":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Approximate Nearest Neighbor Attention (ANNA), an efficient attention mechanism designed to address the quadratic time complexity bottleneck of standard transformers.  ANNA leverages approximate nearest neighbor search to achieve sub-quadratic time complexity.  The paper proves that ANNA-transformers retain the representational power of standard transformers, specifically their ability to simulate Massively Parallel Computation (MPC) algorithms. Further, it demonstrates ANNA's ability to solve key reasoning tasks like Match2 and k-hop with near-optimal depth and that constant depth ANNA-transformers can simulate low-rank transformers. In other words, the paper presents a method for making attention more efficient *without* sacrificing its representational capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies primarily in the introduction of ANNA and its rigorous theoretical analysis within the context of MPC and expressivity. While efficient attention mechanisms are not new, the paper's focus on *provable* retention of expressiveness with a parallel computing framework is a distinctive contribution. The connection with MPC is useful in characterizing the computational nature of the attention mechanism.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to scale transformers to longer sequences.  The theoretical results, connecting ANNA to MPC, imply that ANNA-transformers could be a practical means of leveraging parallel hardware. The empirical results, while limited to relatively small synthetic tasks, offer initial support for the practical viability of ANNA. The theoretical result showing it can simulate low-rank attention is also impactful.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides rigorous mathematical proofs for its claims, especially regarding the equivalence of ANNA-transformers and MPC algorithms. This elevates the work beyond mere empirical observation.
*   **Clear Connection to Existing Work:**  The paper clearly articulates how its work builds upon and extends previous research on transformers, efficient attention, and MPC.
*   **Practical Implications:** The ANNA mechanism offers a concrete alternative to standard attention, with the potential for improved scalability in real-world applications.
*   **Interesting Unified Framework:** The demonstration that constant depth ANNA can simulate constant depth low-rank transformers provides a nice unification of these methods.
*   **Careful Experimental Design:** The authors create the necessary experimental setup and demonstrate strong early results.
*   **Interesting Results:** The theoretical results are strong and clearly stated.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Empirical Evaluation:** The empirical evaluation focuses on small, synthetic datasets. This limits the generalizability of the results and makes it difficult to assess the practical benefits of ANNA in more complex scenarios. It is unclear that the algorithm scales in practice, since ANN is still difficult for large problems.
*   **Complexity of the ANNA Mechanism:** While theoretically sound, the implementation details of ANNA (especially those related to LSH) may be challenging in practice, potentially introducing new bottlenecks. The parameters are difficult to configure.
*   **Focus on Theoretical Equivalence:** While demonstrating theoretical equivalence to MPC is valuable, it doesn't fully address the practical constraints of deploying transformers on modern hardware. Factors such as memory bandwidth and communication overhead are not explicitly considered.
*   **Practical Applicability of the MPC Framework:** MPC is not typically used in practice, so the real world implications of the link to MPC is somewhat unclear.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper possesses significant theoretical strength and a clear potential for practical impact, the limited empirical validation prevents a higher score. The reliance on small synthetic datasets and the inherent complexity of the proposed mechanism are notable drawbacks. A score of 7 reflects the valuable theoretical contribution but acknowledges the need for further empirical investigation to fully realize the potential benefits of ANNA.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### COCO-Urdu: A Large-Scale Urdu Image-Caption Dataset with Multimodal Quality Estimation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09014v1)
- **Authors**: Umair Hassan
- **Abstract**: Urdu, spoken by over 250 million people, remains critically under-served in multimodal and vision-language research. The absence of large-scale, high-quality datasets has limited the development of Urdu-capable systems and reinforced biases in multilingual vision-language models trained primarily on high-resource languages. To address this gap, we present COCO-Urdu, a large-scale image-caption dataset derived from MS COCO, containing 59,000 images and 319,000 Urdu captions selected through stratified sampling to preserve the original distribution. Captions were translated using SeamlessM4T v2 and validated with a hybrid multimodal quality estimation framework that integrates COMET-Kiwi for translation quality, CLIP-based similarity for visual grounding, and BERTScore with back-translation for semantic consistency; low-scoring captions were iteratively refined using open-source large language models. We further benchmark COCO-Urdu on BLEU, SacreBLEU, and chrF, reporting consistently strong results. To the best of our knowledge, COCO-Urdu is the largest publicly available Urdu captioning dataset. By releasing both the dataset and the quality estimation pipeline, we aim to reduce language bias in multimodal research and establish a foundation for inclusive vision-language systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the COCO-Urdu paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces COCO-Urdu, a large-scale image caption dataset in Urdu, derived from the MS COCO dataset. It contains 59,000 images and 319,000 Urdu captions, generated through machine translation using SeamlessM4T v2 and then validated using a novel hybrid multimodal quality estimation (QE) framework. This QE framework incorporates COMET-Kiwi for translation quality, CLIP-based similarity for visual grounding, and BERTScore with back-translation for semantic consistency.  Low-scoring captions are refined using open-source LLMs. The paper also benchmarks COCO-Urdu using BLEU, SacreBLEU, and chrF, and releases both the dataset and the QE pipeline. The aim is to reduce language bias in multimodal research and establish a foundation for inclusive vision-language systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The creation of a large-scale Urdu image caption dataset is, in itself, a novel contribution. While prior Urdu captioning datasets exist (UICD, Flickr8k-based efforts), COCO-Urdu significantly surpasses them in scale and introduces a multimodal quality estimation pipeline that goes beyond simple linguistic evaluation. The hybrid QE framework, combining translation quality, visual grounding, and semantic consistency, is a worthwhile methodological innovation. The stratified sampling to preserve the original MS COCO class distribution is also a good practice. The iterative refinement using LLMs is also a useful and practical aspect of their methodology.

*   **Significance:** Urdu is spoken by over 250 million people, making it a strategically important language for reducing bias in multimodal research. The availability of a large, validated dataset like COCO-Urdu can promote the development of Urdu-capable vision-language systems and improve the generalizability of multilingual models. The release of the QE pipeline is also significant as it provides a reusable tool for evaluating and improving translations in low-resource languages. The benchmarking helps position the dataset and provides baseline performance. The study effectively highlights the underserved nature of Urdu in the research landscape and presents a tangible solution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Scale:** The size of the dataset is a major strength, providing a substantial resource for training and evaluating models.
    *   **Quality Estimation:** The multimodal QE framework addresses a critical gap in existing Urdu captioning datasets, ensuring higher quality translations.
    *   **Open Release:** Releasing both the dataset and the QE pipeline promotes reproducibility and further research.
    *   **Practicality:** The paper addresses practical challenges of low-resource dataset creation, demonstrating a scalable approach.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The steps taken for subset selection, translation, and quality estimation are well-defined and explained.
    *   **QE effectiveness:** The study demonstrated a substantial gain with the refinement step that was targeted through their quality evaluation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Machine Translation Dependence:** While the QE framework mitigates this, the reliance on machine translation as the initial step introduces a potential source of error and bias. More manual checking and validation would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Human Evaluation:** The human evaluation of only 200 captions is a limitation, especially given that the MT+LLM pipeline may introduce subtle errors or cultural insensitivity that are not captured by automatic metrics.
    *   **Reference Translation Methodology**: While using a NLLB-3B model for translation to English provides an automated approach at scale, this could introduce biases into the metrics, impacting the overall reliability of the benchmark.
    *   **Lack of Downstream Task Evaluation**: The paper focuses on translation quality metrics but lacks evaluation on downstream vision-language tasks. Evaluating the performance of models trained on COCO-Urdu on tasks like image retrieval or visual question answering would provide stronger evidence of its utility.
    *   **Generalization claims:** It's important to note that the effectiveness of their chosen weights for the QE components may vary for different languages. The optimal weights should be recalibrated depending on the language.

*   **Impact:** The dataset and QE pipeline have the potential to impact research in Urdu-specific vision-language tasks and cross-lingual transfer learning. It provides a valuable resource for researchers working on reducing bias in multimodal AI.
    *   **Score:** 7

*Rationale*:
The paper presents a significant and novel resource for the Urdu language within the multimodal learning domain. The methodology is sound, particularly the hybrid QE pipeline. However, the score reflects concerns about reliance on machine translation without extensive human validation, and the absence of downstream task evaluations. While the scale and open release are strong positives, the limitations prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### VoxelFormer: Parameter-Efficient Multi-Subject Visual Decoding from fMRI
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09015v1)
- **Authors**: Chenqian Le, Yilin Zhao, Nikasadat Emami, Kushagra Yadav, Xujin "Chris" Liu, Xupeng Chen, Yao Wang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in fMRI-based visual decoding have enabled compelling reconstructions of perceived images. However, most approaches rely on subject-specific training, limiting scalability and practical deployment. We introduce \textbf{VoxelFormer}, a lightweight transformer architecture that enables multi-subject training for visual decoding from fMRI. VoxelFormer integrates a Token Merging Transformer (ToMer) for efficient voxel compression and a query-driven Q-Former that produces fixed-size neural representations aligned with the CLIP image embedding space. Evaluated on the 7T Natural Scenes Dataset, VoxelFormer achieves competitive retrieval performance on subjects included during training with significantly fewer parameters than existing methods. These results highlight token merging and query-based transformers as promising strategies for parameter-efficient neural decoding.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the VoxelFormer paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VoxelFormer, a lightweight transformer architecture designed for parameter-efficient multi-subject visual decoding from fMRI data.  It aims to address the limitations of existing fMRI decoding methods that often rely on large, subject-specific datasets and extensive anatomical alignment. VoxelFormer employs two key components: a Token Merging Transformer (ToMer) to compress raw voxel activations into a compact latent representation, and a query-driven Q-Former to align these representations with the CLIP image embedding space, ensuring a fixed-size representation for multi-subject training. Experiments on the 7T Natural Scenes Dataset demonstrate that VoxelFormer achieves competitive retrieval performance compared to state-of-the-art methods, while using significantly fewer parameters. The paper highlights token merging and query-based transformers as promising strategies for parameter-efficient neural decoding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Parameter Efficiency:** The primary strength lies in its parameter efficiency compared to methods like MindEye1 and MindEye2. Achieving competitive retrieval accuracy with significantly fewer parameters is a valuable contribution, especially in resource-constrained settings or when dealing with large-scale datasets.
    *   **Multi-Subject Training:** The ability to train a single model across multiple subjects without subject-specific layers (as in MindEye2) is a significant advancement. It simplifies deployment and reduces the need for extensive per-subject data.
    *   **Novel Architecture:** The combination of a ToMer and a Q-Former in the context of fMRI decoding is novel. ToMer intelligently reduces the data dimensionality, which would be beneficial when processing fMRI data that is usually high dimensional. Query-based architecture allows to represent multiple subjects in one model.
    *   **Dual-Path training Objective:** Using the dual-path objective is a good strategy, that uses an auxilliary task of alignment with the CLIP embeddings. This could allow training a model that can be used as a prior for diffusion models, which allows reconstruction from brain activity.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Retrieval Accuracy:** While the paper claims "competitive performance", the retrieval accuracy is still *below* state-of-the-art subject-specific approaches (like MindEye2 which achieves >98% accuarcy). The gap in performance needs to be justified considering the model is less complex than MindEye2.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** It would be beneficial to include ablation studies to analyze the individual contribution of ToMer and Q-Former to performance.  This would help demonstrate the effectiveness of each component and justify their integration.
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The paper focuses primarily on retrieval accuracy.  Including other evaluation metrics, such as reconstruction quality or the ability to predict other visual features, would provide a more comprehensive assessment of the model's capabilities. Also, there is a limited number of subjects used in the experiments. Training and testing on more subjects could have increased the robustness and reliability of the results.
    *   **Prior transformer path is not fully utilized:** Although the auxiliary task of the prior transformer pathway is to enable use as the prior for diffusion models, no image reconstruction is done and the model is not assessed on this capability.
    *   **Limited Zero-Shot capability:** The zero-shot retrieval accuracy on subject 1 is significantly worse than if it was a part of training.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The novelty lies in the specific architectural combination (ToMer + Q-Former) for parameter-efficient multi-subject fMRI decoding. While both ToMer and Q-Formers have been used in other domains, their application to fMRI data and the specific design choices made for this context contribute to novelty.
    *   The significance rests on its potential to make fMRI decoding more accessible and scalable. Parameter efficiency is crucial for practical applications, especially with limited computational resources.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could inspire further research into parameter-efficient architectures for fMRI decoding.
    *   It could encourage the exploration of token merging and query-based transformers in other neuroscience applications.
    *   The multi-subject training approach could pave the way for more generalizable and robust brain-computer interfaces.

**Justification for the Score:**

VoxelFormer demonstrates a valuable step towards parameter-efficient multi-subject fMRI decoding. It addresses a significant limitation of existing approaches, showcasing competitive results with significantly reduced model size. While retrieval accuracy remains slightly below state-of-the-art methods, the novel architectural combination and the potential for future improvements warrant a positive evaluation. The weaknesses, such as the lack of ablation studies and more comprehensive evaluation, limit the overall impact of the paper. Overall, the score reflects a solid contribution with clear potential but requiring further refinement.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Integrating Anatomical Priors into a Causal Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09054v1)
- **Authors**: Binxu Li, Wei Peng, Mingjie Li, Ehsan Adeli, Kilian M. Pohl
- **Abstract**: 3D brain MRI studies often examine subtle morphometric differences between cohorts that are hard to detect visually. Given the high cost of MRI acquisition, these studies could greatly benefit from image syntheses, particularly counterfactual image generation, as seen in other domains, such as computer vision. However, counterfactual models struggle to produce anatomically plausible MRIs due to the lack of explicit inductive biases to preserve fine-grained anatomical details. This shortcoming arises from the training of the models aiming to optimize for the overall appearance of the images (e.g., via cross-entropy) rather than preserving subtle, yet medically relevant, local variations across subjects. To preserve subtle variations, we propose to explicitly integrate anatomical constraints on a voxel-level as prior into a generative diffusion framework. Called Probabilistic Causal Graph Model (PCGM), the approach captures anatomical constraints via a probabilistic graph module and translates those constraints into spatial binary masks of regions where subtle variations occur. The masks (encoded by a 3D extension of ControlNet) constrain a novel counterfactual denoising UNet, whose encodings are then transferred into high-quality brain MRIs via our 3D diffusion decoder. Extensive experiments on multiple datasets demonstrate that PCGM generates structural brain MRIs of higher quality than several baseline approaches. Furthermore, we show for the first time that brain measurements extracted from counterfactuals (generated by PCGM) replicate the subtle effects of a disease on cortical brain regions previously reported in the neuroscience literature. This achievement is an important milestone in the use of synthetic MRIs in studies investigating subtle morphological differences.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel diffusion model called Probabilistic Causal Graph Model (PCGM) for generating anatomically plausible 3D brain MRIs, particularly for counterfactual image generation. PCGM integrates anatomical priors at the voxel level by using a probabilistic graph module (PGM) to capture relationships between metadata (e.g., age, sex, diagnosis) and ROI volumes. These relationships are then translated into spatial binary masks using a Counterfactual Mask Generator (CMG). These masks, encoded with a 3D extension of ControlNet, guide a novel counterfactual denoising UNet and a pre-trained 3D diffusion decoder to produce high-quality brain MRIs. The model is evaluated on multiple datasets, demonstrating its ability to generate higher-quality MRIs than baseline approaches. Notably, it is shown that brain measurements extracted from counterfactuals generated by PCGM replicate subtle effects of alcohol use disorder (AUD) on cortical brain regions, a significant achievement not previously attained.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The core novelty lies in the explicit integration of anatomical constraints as voxel-level priors into a diffusion framework for counterfactual brain MRI generation. Prior work has struggled to preserve subtle but medically relevant local variations, and PCGM directly addresses this limitation.
    *   The use of a probabilistic causal graph to encode relationships between metadata and ROI volumes, combined with a mask-guided diffusion process, is also a significant departure from existing approaches.
    *   The 3D ControlNet extension and the dedicated 3D diffusion decoder are further technical contributions.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The ability to generate anatomically plausible counterfactual MRIs has significant implications for neuroscience research and clinical practice. It allows for investigating the potential effects of diseases or interventions on brain structure, even with limited data.
    *   Replicating the subtle effects of AUD on cortical regions is a critical validation of the model's ability to capture meaningful morphological differences and a milestone demonstrating the potential use of synthetic MRIs in clinical studies.
    *   The results highlight the importance of incorporating domain-specific knowledge (i.e., anatomical constraints) into generative models for medical imaging, a potentially impactful paradigm shift.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive evaluation with multiple datasets and comparison to several strong baseline approaches.
    *   The qualitative results demonstrate a clear improvement in image quality and anatomical plausibility, especially in regions like the cerebellum.
    *   Quantitative results (FID, MMD, MS-SSIM) support the superiority of PCGM over baselines.
    *   Successful replication of AUD-related findings on cortical structures provides strong evidence of the model's clinical relevance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on SynthSeg+ for initial segmentation may introduce biases or errors, although the subsequent steps in PCGM appear to mitigate some of these effects.
    *   The computational cost of diffusion models remains a limitation, although the paper does not extensively discuss this aspect.
    *   The method’s reliance on the Desikan-Killiany atlas may not capture more individualized, granular variations in brain morphology.
    *   The comparison uses metrics (FID/MMD) derived from natural image processing, which are useful but may not fully capture the nuances of medical image generation compared to specialist metrics.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in generative models for medical imaging, particularly in the areas of counterfactual generation and disease modeling.
    *   The explicit integration of anatomical priors could become a standard practice in future models.
    *   The ability to generate realistic synthetic data could accelerate research and development in AI-assisted diagnostics and treatment planning.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to a challenging problem in medical image analysis. The explicit incorporation of anatomical priors and causal modeling techniques significantly advances the state-of-the-art in counterfactual brain MRI generation. The experimental results are compelling, and the replication of AUD-related findings demonstrates the model's clinical relevance. The limitations, while present, do not overshadow the significant contributions of the work.

### Enhancing 3D Medical Image Understanding with Pretraining Aided by 2D Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09064v1)
- **Authors**: Qiuhui Chen, Xuancheng Yao, Huping Ye, Yi Hong
- **Abstract**: Understanding 3D medical image volumes is critical in the medical field, yet existing 3D medical convolution and transformer-based self-supervised learning (SSL) methods often lack deep semantic comprehension. Recent advancements in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) provide a promising approach to enhance image understanding through text descriptions. To leverage these 2D MLLMs for improved 3D medical image understanding, we propose Med3DInsight, a novel pretraining framework that integrates 3D image encoders with 2D MLLMs via a specially designed plane-slice-aware transformer module. Additionally, our model employs a partial optimal transport based alignment, demonstrating greater tolerance to noise introduced by potential noises in LLM-generated content. Med3DInsight introduces a new paradigm for scalable multimodal 3D medical representation learning without requiring human annotations. Extensive experiments demonstrate our state-of-the-art performance on two downstream tasks, i.e., segmentation and classification, across various public datasets with CT and MRI modalities, outperforming current SSL methods. Med3DInsight can be seamlessly integrated into existing 3D medical image understanding networks, potentially enhancing their performance. Our source code, generated datasets, and pre-trained models will be available at https://github.com/Qybc/Med3DInsight.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Med3DInsight, a novel pre-training framework designed to enhance 3D medical image understanding by leveraging 2D Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs).  Med3DInsight combines reconstruction-based self-supervised learning with image-text alignment, using a Plane-Slice-Aware Transformer (PSAT) module to bridge the gap between 3D image features and 2D image/text representations. It uses GPT-4V to generate text descriptions for 2D slices extracted from 3D medical images and then aligns these with CLIP embeddings using a Partial Optimal Transport (POT) technique to improve noise tolerance. Extensive experiments across segmentation and classification tasks on various public datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, outperforming existing self-supervised learning (SSL) methods.  The authors also provide code, pre-trained models, and generated datasets for public use.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Positive:** The core novelty lies in effectively bridging the gap between 3D medical imaging and 2D MLLMs. The PSAT module is a novel component that focuses on aligning 3D volume features to corresponding 2D slices, considering spatial orientation, which is a key advancement. Using POT for mitigating noise introduced by LLMs in the context of self-supervised pre-training for medical images is also a significant contribution.
*   **Negative:**  While PSAT module is novel, the overarching architecture leverages well-established concepts like encoder-decoders and attention mechanisms.  The use of GPT-4V for generating descriptions, although effective, is not fundamentally new; other works have explored LLMs in medical imaging, but typically with different alignment and application strategies.

**Significance:**

*   **Positive:** The paper addresses a critical limitation in 3D medical image analysis – the lack of high-level semantic understanding often missing in traditional SSL methods.  By incorporating text descriptions through MLLMs, Med3DInsight pushes towards more clinically relevant and interpretable representations. The consistent state-of-the-art performance across various datasets and tasks shows the practical value of the framework. Releasing the code, models, and generated datasets is highly beneficial for the research community, allowing for further experimentation and development.
*   **Negative:**  The reliance on GPT-4V, while powerful, also introduces a dependency on an external API and potential vulnerability to its biases and inaccuracies.  The improvements observed, while significant, are incremental over existing SSL methods, suggesting that the core challenge of deeply understanding 3D medical images remains a complex problem. Also, due to the LLMs' context limitations to processing each slice independently may affect certain tasks that may rely on a holistic comprehension of the entire 3D volume.

**Justification:**

The paper represents a solid contribution to the field of 3D medical image understanding. The innovative PSAT module and the application of POT for noise reduction are noteworthy.  The experimental results clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of Med3DInsight, and the release of resources is a significant boon to the community.  However, the paper's core architecture relies on established components, and the reliance on external LLM APIs brings potential dependencies and limitations. While substantial and practical, the advance is more incremental than revolutionary. The use of LLMs introduces concerns of relying on black boxes that may contain biases or hallucinate.
Therefore, based on these arguments, a score of 7 is suitable.

### Understanding Economic Tradeoffs Between Human and AI Agents in Bargaining Games
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09071v1)
- **Authors**: Crystal Qian, Kehang Zhu, John Horton, Benjamin S. Manning, Vivian Tsai, James Wexler, Nithum Thain
- **Abstract**: Coordination tasks traditionally performed by humans are increasingly being delegated to autonomous agents. As this pattern progresses, it becomes critical to evaluate not only these agents' performance but also the processes through which they negotiate in dynamic, multi-agent environments. Furthermore, different agents exhibit distinct advantages: traditional statistical agents, such as Bayesian models, may excel under well-specified conditions, whereas large language models (LLMs) can generalize across contexts. In this work, we compare humans (N = 216), LLMs (GPT-4o, Gemini 1.5 Pro), and Bayesian agents in a dynamic negotiation setting that enables direct, identical-condition comparisons across populations, capturing both outcomes and behavioral dynamics. Bayesian agents extract the highest surplus through aggressive optimization, at the cost of frequent trade rejections. Humans and LLMs can achieve similar overall surplus, but through distinct behaviors: LLMs favor conservative, concessionary trades with few rejections, while humans employ more strategic, risk-taking, and fairness-oriented behaviors. Thus, we find that performance parity -- a common benchmark in agent evaluation -- can conceal fundamental differences in process and alignment, which are critical for practical deployment in real-world coordination tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper "Strategic Tradeoffs Between Humans and AI in Multi-Agent Bargaining":

**Summary:**

This paper explores the strategic tradeoffs between humans, Large Language Models (LLMs), and Bayesian agents in a dynamic multi-agent bargaining game. The authors introduce a novel bargaining game where players with private valuations negotiate trades to maximize individual surplus.  They compare the performance and behaviors of human participants, LLM-based agents (GPT-4 and Gemini 1.5 Pro), and specialized Bayesian agents under identical conditions. The study analyzes not just the negotiated outcomes (surplus) but also the processes involved (trading patterns, regret minimization strategies). The key finding is that achieving similar aggregate surplus across different agent types can mask fundamental differences in behavior and alignment with social norms, which have important implications for deploying AI in real-world coordination tasks. Bayesian agents optimize aggressively for surplus, even at the cost of trade rejections. Humans tend to be fair, and LLMs tend to be conservative and concessionary.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its comparative approach, directly evaluating the strategic behavior of humans, LLMs, and statistical models *within the same experimental setting*. This is a significant advance over existing studies that often assess these entities in isolation.
    *   **Experimental Design:** The introduced bargaining game provides a controlled environment that allows for rigorous comparisons and analysis of both outcomes and processes, specifically how different agents navigate trade-offs.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper goes beyond simply reporting surplus achieved. It delves into trading patterns and regret analysis, revealing subtle but important differences in strategic behavior.
    *   **Timeliness and Relevance:** As AI agents increasingly mediate real-world interactions, the study’s insights into alignment with human norms and expectations are highly relevant.
    *   **Strong conclusions**: Conclusions were strongly tied to results, and provided clear insights on each agent and their behavior.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Stylized Bargaining Game:** While a strength in terms of control, the bargaining game is a simplified representation of real-world negotiations. The static valuations, limited action space, and single-shot interactions may not fully capture the complexities of human and LLM strategic reasoning in more dynamic or long-term negotiations.
    *   **Limited LLM Exploration:** Although the LLM analysis employed Chain-of-Thought prompting, it could benefit from further exploration of alternative prompts and fine-tuning strategies, which might uncover more sophisticated strategic behaviors from LLMs. There are multiple approaches to prompts that could be analyzed and potentially yield very different results.
    *   **Generalizability**: The human participant pool may not be fully representative of the population. This is a common limitation in behavioral economics.
    *   **Bayesian agent description**: The bayesian agent design may limit the model behavior and not generalize to other models.

*   **Significance and Impact:**

    *   The paper highlights the limitations of relying solely on outcome-based metrics (e.g., aggregate surplus) when evaluating AI agents in collaborative settings.
    *   It underscores the importance of *procedural alignment* (how agents achieve outcomes) and the need to consider social norms and expectations in AI design.
    *   The findings contribute to a more nuanced understanding of LLM behavior in strategic contexts, suggesting that their tendency towards concessionary trades may not always be optimal.
    *   The research could influence the development of more socially aware and trustworthy AI agents for negotiation and coordination tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a clear contribution by directly comparing human, LLM, and statistical agent strategies in a shared context. The careful experimental design and detailed analysis of both outcomes and process are valuable.  However, the stylized nature of the bargaining game and the potential limitations of the LLM implementation restrain the generalizability of the results. The bayesian model is specifically designed for the game, which limits comparisons and generalizability.

### MR-UIE: Multi-Perspective Reasoning with Reinforcement Learning for Universal Information Extraction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09082v1)
- **Authors**: Zhongqiu Li, Shiquan Wang, Ruiyu Fang, Mengjiao Bao, Zhenhe Wu, Shuangyong Song, Yongxiang Li, Zhongjiang He
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate robust capabilities across diverse research domains. However, their performance in universal information extraction (UIE) remains insufficient, especially when tackling structured output scenarios that involve complex schema descriptions and require multi-step reasoning. While existing approaches enhance the performance of LLMs through in-context learning and instruction tuning, significant limitations nonetheless persist. To enhance the model's generalization ability, we propose integrating reinforcement learning (RL) with multi-perspective reasoning for information extraction (IE) tasks. Our work transitions LLMs from passive extractors to active reasoners, enabling them to understand not only what to extract but also how to reason. Experiments conducted on multiple IE benchmarks demonstrate that MR-UIE consistently elevates extraction accuracy across domains and surpasses state-of-the-art methods on several datasets. Furthermore, incorporating multi-perspective reasoning into RL notably enhances generalization in complex IE tasks, underscoring the critical role of reasoning in challenging scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MR-UIE: Multi-Perspective Reasoning with Reinforcement Learning for Universal Information Extraction":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MR-UIE, a framework designed to enhance Universal Information Extraction (UIE) performance in Large Language Models (LLMs) by integrating multi-perspective reasoning with reinforcement learning (RL).  It addresses the limitations of existing UIE models, particularly in complex scenarios requiring multi-step reasoning and handling ambiguity. MR-UIE operates through four key components: a unified schema representation, a multi-perspective reasoning path construction mechanism (using LLMs to generate diverse reasoning trajectories), adaptive reasoning model training (supervised fine-tuning with intermediate step supervision), and reinforcement learning for reasoning path optimization (rewarding correctness, faithfulness, and efficiency). The authors demonstrate that MR-UIE improves extraction accuracy across various IE benchmarks and enhances generalization in complex tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of existing and new techniques to address a significant problem in UIE. The most novel aspect appears to be the integration of multi-perspective reasoning and reinforcement learning to actively guide the reasoning process of LLMs for information extraction. While existing works have explored RL and reasoning in isolation for IE, the synergy proposed here, with multi-perspective reasoning actively shaping RL's reward signals, seems relatively unexplored. However, the individual components, such as prompting strategies and structured output, have been investigated in other contexts.

*   **Significance:** UIE is a crucial area for making unstructured data accessible and usable. The ability to improve LLM performance on complex IE tasks has broad implications for various applications, from knowledge base construction to automated data analysis.  If MR-UIE proves robust and scalable, it could lead to significant improvements in the accuracy and reliability of information extraction systems. The paper's potential to enable active, context-aware reasoning in LLMs for IE is potentially quite significant.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the shortcomings of current LLM-based UIE models in complex reasoning scenarios.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The MR-UIE framework is well-defined, with a logical progression of components and clear descriptions of each stage.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The experiments cover a range of IE benchmarks, including NER, RE, and EE, with both supervised and zero-shot settings. Ablation studies further demonstrate the contribution of each component.
    *   **Reasonable Justification of Components**: All components are fairly justified based on their rationale.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Large Models:** The method depends on powerful LLMs (Deepseek-R1, Qwen3-8B), which may limit its accessibility and applicability in resource-constrained environments. The reliance on specific model architectures can also be a hindrance.
    *   **Complexity:** The framework involves multiple stages of training and optimization, which can be complex to implement and tune.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Reasoning Paths:** While multi-perspective reasoning is emphasized, there's a lack of detailed analysis on what the different reasoning perspectives are and why they contribute to the performance gain. It would have been stronger if the paper was able to identify a clear understanding of the RL generated reasoning processes.
    *   **Limited discussion on generalization beyond seen perspectives**: This paper claims good improvement with different perspectives, but no discussion is included on what happens if the testing environment has a perspective unseen during training.
    *   **Lack of real-world application evaluation**: The paper has strong theoretical foundation, but it will be more convincing if some evaluations and insights are included.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in UIE by highlighting the importance of active reasoning and the potential of combining multi-perspective approaches with RL. The framework could serve as a template for developing more sophisticated IE systems that can handle complex and ambiguous information.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the reliance on large models and the framework's complexity, the concept of multi-perspective reasoning combined with RL is a promising direction for enhancing UIE in LLMs. The well-defined framework, comprehensive experiments, and clear problem definition warrant a good score. The limitations concerning model dependency, perspective generation insights and limited discussion beyond see perspective, hinder a higher rating.
