### Towards Confidential and Efficient LLM Inference with Dual Privacy Protection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09091v1)
- **Authors**: Honglan Yu, Yibin Wang, Feifei Dai, Dong Liu, Haihui Fan, Xiaoyan Gu
- **Abstract**: CPU-based trusted execution environments (TEEs) and differential privacy (DP) have gained wide applications for private inference. Due to high inference latency in TEEs, researchers use partition-based approaches that offload linear model components to GPUs. However, dense nonlinear layers of large language models (LLMs) result in significant communication overhead between TEEs and GPUs. DP-based approaches apply random noise to protect data privacy, but this compromises LLM performance and semantic understanding. To overcome the above drawbacks, this paper proposes CMIF, a Confidential and efficient Model Inference Framework. CMIF confidentially deploys the embedding layer in the client-side TEE and subsequent layers on GPU servers. Meanwhile, it optimizes the Report-Noisy-Max mechanism to protect sensitive inputs with a slight decrease in model performance. Extensive experiments on Llama-series models demonstrate that CMIF reduces additional inference overhead in TEEs while preserving user data privacy.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper "Towards Confidential and Efficient LLM Inference with Dual Privacy Protection". Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the privacy concerns associated with Large Language Model (LLM) inference, where user data could be exposed to cloud servers.  It proposes CMIF (Confidential and efficient Model Inference Framework), which combines Trusted Execution Environments (TEEs) and differential privacy (DP) to protect data privacy while maintaining inference efficiency. CMIF places the embedding layer (client-side) within a TEE to protect sensitive input tokens. To reduce communication overhead, subsequent layers are executed on GPU servers. The paper introduces an optimized Report-Noisy-Max (RNM) mechanism for text sanitization, replacing sensitive words with semantically similar alternatives while protecting user data privacy. The experimental results showcase improved inference efficiency and data privacy compared to previous TEE-based methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The combination of TEEs and differential privacy is not entirely new. Many prior works have investigated various integration strategies. However, the specific architecture of CMIF, particularly its focus on the embedding layer placement in the TEE *and* the optimized Report-Noisy-Max algorithm, *presents a practical implementation strategy* that appears tailored towards the unique performance bottlenecks and privacy characteristics of LLMs. The paper's focus on optimizing RNM within the TEE context is arguably more novel than the broader concept of using TEEs and DP.  The specific approach of semantic-based input sanitization within a TEE, combined with the architecture, does provide some incremental novelty.
* **Significance:** Privacy in LLM inference is a crucial issue, and the paper tackles a vital problem. The potential to reduce inference overhead in TEE-protected LLMs has substantial practical implications. The work's focus on semantic understanding after sanitization attempts to overcome a key challenge of DP and makes the solution much more usable. The experimental evaluation, focusing on latency and accuracy trade-offs is significant as it attempts to show practical applicability.
* **Strengths:**
    * The paper is well-written and clearly explains the motivations, architecture, and experimental setup.
    * The focus on practical efficiency and the optimization of the RNM mechanism are notable strengths.
    * The experiments are reasonably comprehensive, including different models and datasets.
    * Releasing the code is a strong positive, allowing for independent verification and extensions of the work.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The novelty is somewhat incremental. Prior work has explored TEEs and DP, though perhaps not precisely in this configuration. The novelty relies on the specific configuration/optimizations.
    * The threat model section is brief. A more detailed discussion of potential attacks and limitations of the TEE and DP implementations would improve the robustness of the work. Specifically, a discussion on the limitations of SGX and known vulnerabilities would be valuable.
    * While the experiments include various models and datasets, they are limited to relatively *small* LLMs (Llama2-7B and Llama3-8B), and the transferability to larger models needs to be assessed.
    * The privacy analysis could be more detailed. The sensitivity calculation and DP guarantees should be described more rigorously.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by providing a practical and efficient framework for private LLM inference.  While the overall idea of combining TEE and DP is not entirely novel, CMIF's architecture and specific optimizations, notably the embedding layer placement and optimized RNM implementation, contribute incremental but important advances. The experimental results demonstrate the feasibility of this approach.

However, the paper would benefit from a more detailed privacy analysis, a more robust discussion of potential attacks and the limitations of SGX, and experiments with larger LLMs.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**  The paper achieves a score of 6.5 due to its practical contributions and focus on optimization, but it does have limitations in novelty, privacy analysis rigor, and a limited experimental scope with small LLMs. This is above average but not particularly groundbreaking; the main strength is in the careful application of existing techniques to a relevant problem with practical evaluation. This is a solid engineering effort, rather than a theoretical breakthrough.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### DP-FedLoRA: Privacy-Enhanced Federated Fine-Tuning for On-Device Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09097v1)
- **Authors**: Honghui Xu, Shiva Shrestha, Wei Chen, Zhiyuan Li, Zhipeng Cai
- **Abstract**: As on-device large language model (LLM) systems become increasingly prevalent, federated fine-tuning enables advanced language understanding and generation directly on edge devices; however, it also involves processing sensitive, user-specific data, raising significant privacy concerns within the federated learning framework. To address these challenges, we propose DP-FedLoRA, a privacy-enhanced federated fine-tuning framework that integrates LoRA-based adaptation with differential privacy in a communication-efficient setting. Each client locally clips and perturbs its LoRA matrices using Gaussian noise to satisfy ($\epsilon$, $\delta$)-differential privacy. We further provide a theoretical analysis demonstrating the unbiased nature of the updates and deriving bounds on the variance introduced by noise, offering practical guidance for privacy-budget calibration. Experimental results across mainstream benchmarks show that DP-FedLoRA delivers competitive performance while offering strong privacy guarantees, paving the way for scalable and privacy-preserving LLM deployment in on-device environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DP-FedLoRA, a privacy-enhanced federated fine-tuning framework for on-device large language models (LLMs). DP-FedLoRA combines Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) with differential privacy (DP) to enable efficient and privacy-preserving LLM adaptation on edge devices.  Each client locally clips and perturbs LoRA matrices using Gaussian noise to ensure (ε, δ)-differential privacy. The paper provides theoretical analysis demonstrating the unbiased nature of updates and deriving variance bounds.  Experiments across benchmarks demonstrate that DP-FedLoRA achieves competitive performance while providing strong privacy guarantees.  The framework includes a noise-injected aggregation mechanism designed for heterogeneous clients and LoRA structures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a reasonable level of novelty. While federated learning with differential privacy and LoRA are not entirely new concepts, their specific combination for on-device LLM fine-tuning, along with a specially designed aggregation mechanism and theoretical analysis, constitutes a novel contribution. The key lies in the adaptation of existing techniques to the unique challenges of resource-constrained edge devices and privacy-sensitive LLM applications. The structured aggregation operation aimed at privacy and heterogeneous client settings is a noteworthy element.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work is solid.  Privacy concerns in federated learning, particularly when applied to LLMs processing sensitive user data, are a major hurdle to real-world deployment.  DP-FedLoRA directly addresses this concern by providing a framework with formal privacy guarantees. The potential for widespread adoption of on-device LLMs increases significantly with the availability of privacy-preserving adaptation techniques. The theoretical analysis strengthens the work and provides practical guidance for parameter tuning.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper addresses both practical design and theoretical analysis.
    *   **Relevant Problem:** It tackles a very relevant and pressing issue in the current landscape of on-device LLMs.
    *   **Clear Explanation:** The paper is generally well-written and explains the approach clearly.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The experiments, including ablations and comparisons against baselines, provide empirical support for the claims.
    *   **Addresses Heterogeneity:** The method's ability to handle heterogeneous LoRA ranks is a practical advantage.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Performance Impact:** The reported performance is competitive, but it's important to acknowledge that differential privacy inherently introduces a performance cost. A more detailed analysis of the performance-privacy trade-off, including varying levels of privacy guarantees (different ε and δ values) and their impact on accuracy and convergence speed, would further strengthen the paper.
    *   **Theoretical Depth:** While the theoretical analysis is a plus, it could be strengthened by considering more sophisticated privacy accounting methods like Rényi differential privacy, which might provide tighter bounds.
    *   **Threat Model Assumptions:** The paper assumes a semi-honest server. A brief discussion of the limitations of this assumption and potential defenses against stronger adversaries would be useful.

*   **Potential Impact:** DP-FedLoRA has the potential to influence the development and deployment of privacy-preserving on-device LLM applications. It provides a practical and theoretically grounded framework that can be used as a foundation for future research in this area. The noise-injected aggregation and heteroheneous rank handling parts of this framework should allow practitioners to quickly adopt it to their systems.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the paper provides a novel combination of existing techniques and addresses a relevant problem with strong experimental validation, the performance impact of DP and theoretical privacy accounting aspects merit more thorough consideration and could serve as a base for further studies.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### TigerCoder: A Novel Suite of LLMs for Code Generation in Bangla
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09101v1)
- **Authors**: Nishat Raihan, Antonios Anastasopoulos, Marcos Zampieri
- **Abstract**: Despite being the 5th most spoken language, Bangla remains underrepresented in Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly for code generation. This primarily stems from the scarcity of high-quality data to pre-train and/or finetune such models. Hence, we introduce the first dedicated family of Code LLMs for Bangla (1B & 9B). We offer three major contributions: (1) a comprehensive Bangla code instruction datasets for programming domain adaptation; (2) MBPP-Bangla, an evaluation benchmark for Bangla code generation; and (3) the TigerCoder-family of Code LLMs, achieving significant ~11-18% performance gains at Pass@1 over existing multilingual and general-purpose Bangla LLMs. Our findings show that curated, high-quality datasets can overcome limitations of smaller models for low-resource languages. We open-source all resources to advance further Bangla LLM research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TigerCoder, a new family of code-generating Large Language Models (LLMs) specifically designed for the Bangla language.  The authors address the under-representation of Bangla in code generation by creating a comprehensive Bangla code instruction dataset, an evaluation benchmark called MBPP-Bangla, and the TigerCoder LLMs themselves (1B and 9B parameters).  The paper claims significant performance gains (11-18% Pass@1) over existing multilingual and general-purpose Bangla LLMs, highlighting the importance of curated, high-quality data for low-resource languages. The resources are open-sourced to foster further research. They also conduct experiments examining prompting in Bangla versus translating Bangla to English before prompting.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper introduces a truly novel resource. The dedicated Bangla code generation LLM, along with its training data and evaluation benchmark (MBPP-Bangla), constitutes a significant and unique contribution. Addressing the lack of resources for Bangla code generation is commendable. The study of prompting in Bangla vs MT-augmented English prompting is also interesting.
*   **Weaknesses:** While the individual components are valuable, the *overall* approach (fine-tuning a smaller model on curated data) is not groundbreaking in the broader LLM landscape. Similar techniques have been used successfully for other languages and tasks. The core idea of "dedicated dataset improves performance" is well-established. Also, there are other Bangla LLMs, the paper should address the differences more.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:** The work directly addresses a significant gap in LLM research and development: the lack of support for low-resource languages in code generation. Making these resources publicly available can have a real-world impact, enabling new applications and research in Bangla-speaking communities. This has the potential to democratize access to AI-powered software development. The reported performance improvements (11-18% Pass@1) are substantial and practically meaningful. The work highlights the continued importance of data-centric AI, showing that high-quality data can compensate for smaller model size.
*   **Weaknesses:** The evaluation is limited to Pass@K, which may not fully capture the nuances of code generation quality (e.g., readability, maintainability, security). The authors acknowledge this and perhaps future work could address other metrics. The paper focuses on code generation but does not discuss the broader implications for software engineering workflows, developer productivity, or the social impact of code generation in Bangla-speaking regions. The baselines compared may not be comprehensive.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a clear and valuable contribution to the field by addressing the under-representation of Bangla in code generation LLMs. It introduces new resources and demonstrates significant performance improvements. However, the overall approach is not entirely novel in the broader context of LLM research and the evaluation is limited in scope. While practically relevant and of clear benefit to Bangla speakers, the impact may be somewhat niche. The claims are clearly presented and well-supported by experimental evidence, enhancing the credibility of this work.

### Character-Level Perturbations Disrupt LLM Watermarks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09112v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaoxi Zhang, Xiaomei Zhang, Yanjun Zhang, He Zhang, Shirui Pan, Bo Liu, Asif Qumer Gill, Leo Yu Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Model (LLM) watermarking embeds detectable signals into generated text for copyright protection, misuse prevention, and content detection. While prior studies evaluate robustness using watermark removal attacks, these methods are often suboptimal, creating the misconception that effective removal requires large perturbations or powerful adversaries. To bridge the gap, we first formalize the system model for LLM watermark, and characterize two realistic threat models constrained on limited access to the watermark detector. We then analyze how different types of perturbation vary in their attack range, i.e., the number of tokens they can affect with a single edit. We observe that character-level perturbations (e.g., typos, swaps, deletions, homoglyphs) can influence multiple tokens simultaneously by disrupting the tokenization process. We demonstrate that character-level perturbations are significantly more effective for watermark removal under the most restrictive threat model. We further propose guided removal attacks based on the Genetic Algorithm (GA) that uses a reference detector for optimization. Under a practical threat model with limited black-box queries to the watermark detector, our method demonstrates strong removal performance. Experiments confirm the superiority of character-level perturbations and the effectiveness of the GA in removing watermarks under realistic constraints. Additionally, we argue there is an adversarial dilemma when considering potential defenses: any fixed defense can be bypassed by a suitable perturbation strategy. Motivated by this principle, we propose an adaptive compound character-level attack. Experimental results show that this approach can effectively defeat the defenses. Our findings highlight significant vulnerabilities in existing LLM watermark schemes and underline the urgency for the development of new robust mechanisms.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Character-Level Perturbations Disrupt LLM Watermarks" investigates the robustness of LLM watermarking schemes against adversarial attacks. It argues that existing removal attacks are suboptimal and overestimate the robustness of watermarks. The paper introduces the idea that character-level perturbations (typos, deletions, etc.) are more effective than token-level or sentence-level perturbations because they disrupt tokenization and affect a larger range of tokens simultaneously. The paper also proposes a guided removal attack using a Genetic Algorithm (GA) and a reference detector to prioritize impactful token positions for perturbation under realistic black-box query constraints. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of their approach across various watermarking schemes and LLMs. Finally, it proposes an adaptive compound attack to overcome potential defenses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in systematically analyzing and exploiting the impact of character-level perturbations for watermark removal. While character-level edits have been used in adversarial NLP before, their specific application to LLM watermarks and their mechanism of disrupting tokenization is a novel insight. Furthermore, the use of a GA-guided search for effective perturbations under limited query budgets and the proposal of an adaptive attack strategy is also a significant contribution. It points out the adversarial dilemma, a weakness in watermarking's design and defense.
*   **Significance:** The paper has high significance because it reveals a significant vulnerability in existing LLM watermarking schemes, questioning their effectiveness in realistic threat models. The findings underscore the importance of rigorously evaluating watermarking schemes against a wider range of attacks and under realistic constraints. The work also highlights the need for novel, more robust watermarking mechanisms and defenses. The proposed attack methods provide a practical framework for assessing the robustness of new and existing watermarking schemes.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Systematic and well-defined problem formulation.
    *   Clear characterization of threat models.
    *   Strong experimental evaluation across diverse watermarking schemes and LLMs.
    *   Insightful analysis of character-level perturbations and their impact on tokenization.
    *   Addresses limitations of existing watermark removal methods.
    *   Well-organized and clearly written.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper primarily focuses on *inference-time* watermarks. While the most practical, it does not explore the vulnerability of more robust, *training-time* watermarks.
    *   While the GA-based approach is effective, the training of reference detectors relies on collecting data using limited queries, which could affect the accuracy of the attack evaluation. The accuracy of the reference detector needs better quantification and analysis.
    *   The study focuses primarily on text. The conclusions might not directly translate to other modalities like images or audio.
    *   The adaptive attack targets particular defenses; there is little discussion of defenses beyond spellcheck, OCR, or Unicode normalization.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on LLM watermarking by prompting the development of more robust schemes resistant to character-level attacks. It will also encourage more realistic and rigorous threat modeling in evaluating watermark robustness. The proposed GA-based removal attack and adaptive strategies will serve as valuable tools for assessing and improving watermarking schemes. The analysis of character-level perturbations is already informing a rethinking of attack surfaces.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by unveiling a previously underappreciated weakness in LLM watermarking techniques. The systematic approach, well-defined threat models, and thorough experimental evaluation strengthen the paper's conclusions. However, the limitations regarding the scope of watermarking methods, the evaluation of reference detectors, and the limited defense strategies considered prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the paper has some weaknesses, its impact on the field is substantial, pushing the research community toward more robust and practically relevant watermarking approaches.

### Sensitivity-LoRA: Low-Load Sensitivity-Based Fine-Tuning for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09119v1)
- **Authors**: Hao Zhang, Bo Huang, Zhenjia Li, Xi Xiao, Hui Yi Leong, Zumeng Zhang, Xinwei Long, Tianyang Wang, Hao Xu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have transformed both everyday life and scientific research. However, adapting LLMs from general-purpose models to specialized tasks remains challenging, particularly in resource-constrained environments. Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA), a prominent method within Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT), has emerged as a promising approach to LLMs by approximating model weight updates using low-rank decomposition. However, LoRA is limited by its uniform rank ( r ) allocation to each incremental matrix, and existing rank allocation techniques aimed at addressing this issue remain computationally inefficient, complex, and unstable, hindering practical applications. To address these limitations, we propose Sensitivity-LoRA, an efficient fine-tuning method that dynamically allocates ranks to weight matrices based on both their global and local sensitivities. It leverages the second-order derivatives (Hessian Matrix) of the loss function to effectively capture weight sensitivity, enabling optimal rank allocation with minimal computational overhead. Our experimental results have demonstrated robust effectiveness, efficiency and stability of Sensitivity-LoRA across diverse tasks and benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Sensitivity-LoRA, a parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) method designed to dynamically allocate ranks in Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). Unlike standard LoRA, which uses a uniform rank for all weight matrices, Sensitivity-LoRA allocates ranks based on both global and local sensitivities of those matrices.  It leverages the Hessian matrix (second-order derivatives of the loss function) to measure these sensitivities.  By considering both global (trace of the Hessian) and local (TopK and Effective Rank) sensitivity measures, the method aims to optimize rank allocation, reducing computational overhead and enhancing training stability compared to existing dynamic rank allocation techniques. The paper provides experimental results across NLU and NLG tasks demonstrating improved performance over baselines and demonstrating efficiency and stability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies primarily in the efficient implementation of dynamic rank allocation using the Hessian matrix. While dynamic rank allocation isn't entirely new, the use of second-order derivatives to estimate sensitivity and the specific combination of global and local sensitivity measures offer a unique approach. The claim of low computational overhead is a significant contribution if demonstrably true, as this has been a bottleneck in previous work in the area. The approach also stands out because it prioritizes stability during training, which is a concern for other strategies.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of fine-tuning large language models, especially in resource-constrained settings. PEFT methods are critical for broader accessibility to LLM customization, and an improvement in dynamic rank allocation would enhance that potential.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper introduces a computationally efficient way to estimate weight sensitivity.
    *   The integration of both global and local sensitivity measures for a more nuanced rank allocation.
    *   The claim of improved stability in training, an important practical consideration.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across a diverse range of tasks and benchmarks.
    * The authors provide ablations, analyzing the impact of the local and global components and different rank allocation methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the experiments cover a reasonable range of tasks, the reliance on certain model architectures and benchmarks could limit the generalizability of the findings. Expanding experiments to larger models would strengthen these claims.
    *  A key promise is the "low-load" nature and reduced computational burden. It is necessary to see detailed ablation studies involving computational costs, memory, and training duration compared against the baseline methods.
    *  The performance gains, while consistently positive, are sometimes marginal.
    *   The paper could benefit from a deeper theoretical analysis of the method, potentially explaining why the specific combination of sensitivity measures leads to better performance.
    * The paper could be significantly enhanced by open-sourcing the code, enabling verification of the results and further development by the community.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the claims regarding efficiency and stability are substantiated, Sensitivity-LoRA could become a prominent method for parameter-efficient fine-tuning, particularly in scenarios where resource constraints are a concern. It could spur further research into sensitivity-based rank allocation and optimization. The work is directly applicable to current industry practices.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. The paper introduces a reasonably novel and potentially significant approach to dynamic rank allocation in LoRA. While it has some weaknesses and areas for improvement, the strengths (efficiency, stability, the integration of global/local sensitivity) warrant a positive assessment. The significance hinges on the validity of the claims regarding low overhead and robust performance. Future research should focus on verifying these claims more thoroughly, expanding the experiments to more complex models and tasks, and releasing code to facilitate reproducibility and further exploration.

### Compass-v3: Scaling Domain-Specific LLMs for Multilingual E-Commerce in Southeast Asia
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09121v1)
- **Authors**: Sophia Maria
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) excel in general-domain applications, yet their performance often degrades in specialized tasks requiring domain-specific knowledge. E-commerce is particularly challenging, as its data are noisy, heterogeneous, multilingual, and highly dynamic. We present Compass-v3, a vertical-domain Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model with 245B total parameters and 71B active per token, designed for Southeast Asian e-commerce. Compass-v3 adopts fewer but larger experts, combined with hardware-efficient optimizations-such as intra-node expert parallelism and a customized memcpy operator-to maximize GPU utilization. The model is trained on 12T tokens of curated multilingual corpora and large-scale synthetic e-commerce instructions using a mixed-training strategy. To enhance alignment, we propose Optimal-Transport Direct Preference Optimization (OTPO), which captures token-level distinctions and improves instruction adherence in commerce-specific scenarios. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that Compass-v3 delivers state-of-the-art e-commerce performance, surpassing DeepSeek-V3.1, GPT-4 series, and Qwen3-235B. Moreover, Compass-v3 demonstrates strong multilingual capability across low-resource Southeast Asian languages (Indonesian, Thai, Filipino, Vietnamese, Malay, Taglog) and Portuguese while sustaining competitive performance on general benchmarks. It has already been widely applied in Shopee's industrial-scale e-commerce platform and is gradually replacing OpenAI's traffic, now accounting for over 70\% of total LLM usage, highlighting its dual strengths in specialized commerce expertise and broad linguistic competence.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper, "Compass-v3: Scaling Domain-Specific LLMs for Multilingual E-Commerce in Southeast Asia":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Compass-v3, a 245B parameter (71B active) Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language model specifically designed for Southeast Asian (SEA) e-commerce.  The model aims to address the challenges of noisy, heterogeneous, multilingual, and dynamic data inherent in e-commerce. Compass-v3 incorporates fewer but larger experts in its MoE architecture, combined with hardware-efficient optimizations (intra-node expert parallelism, memcpy improvements, expert-aware FP8 quantization) to maximize GPU utilization. It's trained on a 12T token corpus of curated multilingual data and synthetic e-commerce instructions and aligned using a novel Optimal-Transport Direct Preference Optimization (OTPO) technique. Extensive evaluations demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on both in-house and open-source e-commerce benchmarks, along with strong multilingual capabilities across low-resource SEA languages, without sacrificing general performance. The paper highlights the model's successful deployment at Shopee.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The focus on a vertical domain (SEA e-commerce) is valuable and timely given the unique characteristics of the region. The deliberate focus on low-resource SEA languages distinguishes the work from those focused mainly on English and Chinese.
    *   The proposed Optimal-Transport Direct Preference Optimization (OTPO) presents a potentially novel alignment technique, addressing token-level distinctions in preference.
    *   The hardware-aware optimizations, particularly the expert-aware FP8 quantization scheme, are practical and valuable for deploying large MoE models in resource-constrained environments.
    *   The carefully curated and synthetic instruction dataset, especially the incorporation of business-critical workflows, adds practical value.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the individual components (MoE, data curation, RL alignment) are not entirely novel, their combination and adaptation to the SEA e-commerce domain have elements of novelty.
    *   The description of OTPO, while present, lacks some depth and might require more theoretical backing or analysis to fully establish its novelty and advantages.  The paper would benefit from a more detailed discussion of how OTPO compares to existing DPO variants and what specific problems in e-commerce it uniquely solves.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The development of a domain-specific LLM that demonstrably outperforms general-purpose models and strong open-source baselines on relevant e-commerce tasks is significant. The performance gains on in-house datasets are especially compelling, suggesting real-world applicability.
    *   The success of Compass-v3’s deployment within Shopee's platform and its replacement of a significant portion of OpenAI traffic demonstrates practical impact and business value.
    *   The emphasis on multilingual support for low-resource languages makes the model particularly important for the SEA region.
    *   The resource-efficient framework, including training and inference optimizations, promotes broader accessibility and deployment of large models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dependence on Shopee-internal data and benchmarks, while realistic, limits the replicability and generalizability of some of the results. More comprehensive evaluation on widely-used open datasets could strengthen the claims.
    *   While the paper highlights the architecture and training process, some aspects, particularly the data curation and synthetic data generation, could be described in more detail to allow for better reproducibility and adoption by other researchers.
    *   The model's general language abilities appear to be secondary to the vertical domain proficiency, and so it might not be a highly valuable contribution to general LLM development.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of domain-specific language models, specifically tailored to the unique challenges of Southeast Asian e-commerce. The combination of architectural innovations, hardware-aware optimizations, carefully curated data, and a novel alignment technique (OTPO) has resulted in a demonstrably effective model that has been successfully deployed in a real-world industrial setting. The focus on low-resource SEA languages makes the work particularly relevant for the region.

However, the novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary, with the components being largely adaptations of existing techniques. Also, reliance on closed datasets and the lack of detailed description for OTPO affect its replicability and generalizability. The limited detail of the data collection strategies, and the exact mechanism behind the effectiveness of OTPO, and lack of more publicly available benchmarks slightly diminishes the paper's overall impact in the broader AI community.

Taking these considerations into account, a score of 7 reflects the paper's valuable and impactful contribution balanced with its limitations in novelty, replicability, and generalizability.
