Strategically combining EP and TP based on expert activation frequency is a logical approach to balance communication and computation. The dynamic scheduling component adds another layer of optimization to adapt to the inherently dynamic nature of MoE workloads. The online strategy to predict computation load, prioritize experts, and pre-broadcast the experts is a novel approach for these kind of architectures.
*   **Performance Analytical Model:** The development of a performance model that considers both computation and communication costs is a valuable contribution. This model allows for better understanding and optimization of the parallel mapping strategy. The simulator to model irregular all-to-all communication adds another dimension to estimate the overheads of using the NMP architecture.
*   **Experimental Evaluation:** The experimental results demonstrate performance improvements over baseline approaches (TP, EP, and hybrid TP-EP).  The ablation study provides insights into the effectiveness of the individual components (Node Balance, Link Balance, and Dynamic Scheduling) of HD-MoE. The use of established MoE models (Mixtral, DeepSeek, Qwen) and performance metrics (TBT latency) adds credibility to the evaluation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity:** While the hybrid approach seems logical, the overall complexity of the proposed algorithm appears relatively high with multiple levels of optimization. The paper might benefit from more discussion on the overhead of the dynamic scheduling component. There is an inherent trade-off on the number of experts being pre-broadcast given the limitation on bandwidth, while having a larger number of experts being pre-broadcast will lead to reduced latency.
*   **Generality:** The evaluation is limited to three MoE models (Mixtral, DeepSeek, and Qwen). While these are significant models, it would be beneficial to demonstrate the effectiveness of HD-MoE across a wider range of MoE architectures with diverse expert activation patterns.
*   **Implementation details:** More details about the implementation of the simulation environment and parameter settings is necessary. This would make the paper more reproducible.
*   **Limited scope of hardware configurations:** The paper uses just three hardware configurations, limiting the generalizability of the analysis.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper offers a practical solution to deploying MoE models on 3D NMP architectures. The approach of combining hybrid parallelism with dynamic scheduling is novel. While some previous works have explored dynamic scheduling, the specific combination with hybrid parallelism and the targeting of 3D NMP architectures distinguishes this work. The performance gains demonstrated in the experiments are significant and suggest that HD-MoE could be a valuable tool for practitioners working with MoE models on NMP platforms.

However, the evaluation has some limitations and there are aspects that limit the generality of the proposed method. The novelty is moderate, while the practicality is high.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM deployment on emerging hardware. The HD-MoE framework addresses a practical problem, and the experimental results show promising performance improvements. However, the limited evaluation scope (small set of models and limited evaluation hardware) and the overall complexity of the approach, the necessity of developing a model tailored specifically to the 3D NMP paradigm, limits the score. It is still a significant contribution to the field, meriting a score of 7.

- **Classification**: cs.PF
- **Score**: 7/10

### ENSI: Efficient Non-Interactive Secure Inference for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09424v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyu He, Maojiang Wang, Xinwen Gao, Yuchuan Luo, Lin Liu, Shaojing Fu
- **Abstract**: Secure inference enables privacy-preserving machine learning by leveraging cryptographic protocols that support computations on sensitive user data without exposing it. However, integrating cryptographic protocols with large language models (LLMs) presents significant challenges, as the inherent complexity of these protocols, together with LLMs' massive parameter scale and sophisticated architectures, severely limits practical usability. In this work, we propose ENSI, a novel non-interactive secure inference framework for LLMs, based on the principle of co-designing the cryptographic protocols and LLM architecture. ENSI employs an optimized encoding strategy that seamlessly integrates CKKS scheme with a lightweight LLM variant, BitNet, significantly reducing the computational complexity of encrypted matrix multiplications. In response to the prohibitive computational demands of softmax under homomorphic encryption (HE), we pioneer the integration of the sigmoid attention mechanism with HE as a seamless, retraining-free alternative. Furthermore, by embedding the Bootstrapping operation within the RMSNorm process, we efficiently refresh ciphertexts while markedly decreasing the frequency of costly bootstrapping invocations. Experimental evaluations demonstrate that ENSI achieves approximately an 8x acceleration in matrix multiplications and a 2.6x speedup in softmax inference on CPU compared to state-of-the-art method, with the proportion of bootstrapping is reduced to just 1%.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ENSI: Efficient Non-Interactive Secure Inference for Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ENSI, a novel framework for efficient non-interactive secure inference of large language models (LLMs). It addresses the challenges of integrating homomorphic encryption (HE) with LLMs by co-designing the cryptographic protocols and the LLM architecture. ENSI uses an optimized encoding strategy that integrates the CKKS scheme with a lightweight LLM variant (BitNet), reducing the complexity of encrypted matrix multiplications.  It also integrates the sigmoid attention mechanism as a retraining-free alternative to softmax, and embeds the Bootstrapping operation within the RMSNorm process to refresh ciphertexts efficiently.  The experimental results show significant speedups compared to the state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates novelty on several fronts:
    *   **Co-design Approach:** The idea of jointly optimizing the LLM architecture and the encryption scheme is a valuable contribution. This holistic approach is essential for practical secure inference of LLMs, which often struggle with individual optimizations alone.
    *   **BitNet Integration:** The adaptation of BitNet, a quantized LLM, for secure inference with HE is a clever way to reduce computational overhead. While BitNet itself isn't new, its specific use in secure inference within the ENSI framework is a novel application.
    *   **Sigmoid Attention Replacement:** Substituting softmax with sigmoid attention and integrating it seamlessly without retraining is a significant practical contribution. Softmax is known to be computationally expensive under HE, and finding a viable replacement is a key advancement.
    *   **Bootstrapping Optimization:**  The integration of bootstrapping within RMSNorm to reduce its frequency is a valuable optimization technique specifically tailored for the ENSI framework.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem: the impracticality of secure inference for resource-intensive LLMs. If the performance improvements claimed hold up in real-world deployments, this research has the potential to significantly impact the adoption of privacy-preserving LLM services. The specific innovations (co-design, BitNet adaptation, sigmoid attention, and bootstrapping optimization) contribute to a more usable and efficient secure inference framework.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper tackles several key bottlenecks in secure LLM inference (matrix multiplication, softmax, bootstrapping), offering a multi-faceted solution.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The emphasis on a retraining-free softmax replacement and minimizing bootstrapping demonstrates a commitment to practical usability.
    *   **Clear Experimental Results:** The paper provides experimental evidence to support the performance claims, comparing ENSI against other state-of-the-art methods.
    *   **Open-Source Code:** Providing accessible open-source code enhances the reproducibility and adoption of the research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Accuracy Evaluation:** The zero-shot accuracy comparison is limited to three datasets (PIQA, COPA, SST), and it would benefit from more extensive evaluation on a wider range of tasks and datasets.
    *   **Lack of Security Analysis Details:** While the paper mentions being secure in the honest-but-curious model, it would strengthen the paper to include a more detailed security analysis justifying the choices of parameters and the protocol design.
    *   **Scalability Considerations:** Though promising, the framework's performance characteristics on extremely large models beyond 700M parameters (and the associated resource implications) aren't fully explored.
    *   **Comparison with alternative secure attention methods** The paper should compare against methods like BRP-max from PowerFormer in terms of computational cost in HE and accuracy, and discuss its advantages.
    *   **Alternative HE protocols**: The comparison is done with other methods using RNS-CKKS, and should ideally discuss how the framework would adapt to other HE protocols.
    *   **Code implementation and release:** The code is not yet publicly available. This would allow community evaluation.

*   **Potential Influence:** If validated and further improved, the ENSI framework could become a building block for privacy-preserving LLM services. The specific techniques (co-design with quantized models, sigmoid attention, bootstrapping optimization) could be adopted and adapted by other researchers in the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

ENSI represents a significant step forward in making secure LLM inference more practical. The co-design approach and specific optimizations contribute to real performance improvements. The limitations regarding accuracy evaluation and more detailed security analysis prevent a higher score. The score reflects the paper's potential influence on the field, provided the proposed framework can be scaled and further refined. Specifically, it requires the authors to release their source code for scrutiny by the community and perform in-depth experiments on larger and more diverse benchmark datasets. Furthermore, the security analysis could be improved.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### GrACE: A Generative Approach to Better Confidence Elicitation in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09438v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaohan Zhang, Ziquan Liu, Ioannis Patras
- **Abstract**: Assessing the reliability of Large Language Models (LLMs) by confidence elicitation is a prominent approach to AI safety in high-stakes applications, such as healthcare and finance. Existing methods either require expensive computational overhead or suffer from poor calibration, making them impractical and unreliable for real-world deployment. In this work, we propose GrACE, a Generative Approach to Confidence Elicitation that enables scalable and reliable confidence elicitation for LLMs. GrACE adopts a novel mechanism in which the model expresses confidence by the similarity between the last hidden state and the embedding of a special token appended to the vocabulary, in real-time. We fine-tune the model for calibrating the confidence with calibration targets associated with accuracy. Experiments with three LLMs and two benchmark datasets show that the confidence produced by GrACE achieves the best discriminative capacity and calibration on open-ended generation tasks, outperforming six competing methods without resorting to additional sampling or an auxiliary model. Moreover, we propose two strategies for improving test-time scaling based on confidence induced by GrACE. Experimental results show that using GrACE not only improves the accuracy of the final decision but also significantly reduces the number of required samples in the test-time scaling scheme, indicating the potential of GrACE as a practical solution for deploying LLMs with scalable, reliable, and real-time confidence estimation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GrACE: A Generative Approach to Better Confidence Elicitation in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GrACE, a novel and efficient method for confidence elicitation in large language models (LLMs). Unlike existing methods that rely on computationally expensive post-generation processing or struggle with poor calibration, GrACE integrates confidence estimation directly into the generation process. It achieves this by adding a special token `<CNF>` to the vocabulary and training the model to express confidence through the similarity between the last hidden state and the embedding of this token. A calibration process aligns this confidence score with the actual accuracy. The paper demonstrates GrACE's superior discriminative capacity and calibration compared to other methods on open-ended generation tasks, and it shows how GrACE can improve test-time scaling (TTS) strategies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of GrACE – embedding confidence estimation within the generative process using a special token and similarity measure – is indeed novel. Existing post-generation methods add overhead, and verbalized confidence is limited in precision. GrACE avoids both these problems. The calibration strategy, while using k-fold binning (an established technique) and applying a linear probe (also quite standard), leverages them in the *context* of the new confidence elicitation mechanism, which can be considered a valuable extension.

*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in the potential to deploy LLMs in real-world, high-stakes applications where reliability is crucial. If GrACE truly enables scalable, reliable, and real-time confidence estimation, it could significantly impact AI safety and trust. Furthermore, the improved TTS strategies presented will be crucial in optimizing performance with the current computing power.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Efficiency:** The "on-the-fly" nature of GrACE's confidence elicitation is a major advantage, avoiding the overhead of post-generation analysis.
    *   **Calibration and Discrimination:** The paper demonstrates, through extensive experiments, that GrACE outperforms existing methods in both calibration and discrimination. The experiments on multiple model types also ensure reliability.
    *   **TTS Integration:**  The successful integration of GrACE with TTS strategies further highlights its practicality and value in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Generalizability:**  The paper showed strong generalizability to unseen domains, which makes it more reliable in deployment.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Linear Probe:** The reliance on a linear probe, even for calibration, introduces a degree of dependence on the LLM's internal representations. While the experiments show good results, there is a risk that GrACE's effectiveness could be diminished for models with significantly different architectures or training regimes.
    *   **Single Token Limitations:** As the paper mentioned, the confidence score given is overall confidence. This is less useful for Chain-of-Thought or other complicated reasoning processes, because the steps could have different confidences.
    *   **Black-box Evaluation:** The performance is evaluated without any insight of why one case would result in higher or lower confidence. The model internal is treated as a black box that cannot be explained.

*   **Potential Influence:**  GrACE could influence future research in confidence elicitation by:
    *   Encouraging more exploration of integrated, generative approaches.
    *   Serving as a benchmark for evaluating new confidence estimation methods.
    *   Inspiring further development of TTS strategies that leverage confidence information.

**Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **7**.

*   The core idea is novel and tackles a significant challenge in the deployment of LLMs.
*   The experimental results strongly support the claim of superior performance compared to existing methods.
*   The TTS integration demonstrates the practical utility of GrACE.
*   There are limitations regarding the reliance on linear probes, the "single token" score, and lack of interpretability of internal states, which, while not fatal, prevent a higher score. It's also worth mentioning that k-fold binning and calibration with a linear probe, while part of a successful system, are not individually breakthrough contributions. The main novelty lies in the real-time elicitation process itself.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TORSO: Template-Oriented Reasoning Towards General Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09448v1)
- **Authors**: Minhyuk Kim, Seungyoon Lee, Heuiseok Lim
- **Abstract**: The approaches that guide Large Language Models (LLMs) to emulate human reasoning during response generation have emerged as an effective method for enabling them to solve complex problems in a step-by-step manner, thereby achieving superior performance. However, most existing approaches using few-shot prompts to generate responses heavily depend on the provided examples, limiting the utilization of the model's inherent reasoning capabilities. Moreover, constructing task-specific few-shot prompts is often costly and may lead to inconsistencies across different tasks. In this work, we introduce Template-Oriented Reasoning (TORSO), which elicits the model to utilize internal reasoning abilities to generate proper responses across various tasks without the need for manually crafted few-shot examples. Our experimental results demonstrate that TORSO achieves strong performance on diverse LLMs benchmarks with reasonable rationales.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

The paper introduces Template-Oriented Reasoning (TORSO), a method for guiding Large Language Models (LLMs) to utilize their internal reasoning abilities without relying on task-specific few-shot examples or additional training data. TORSO uses logit processing to inject specific tokens at the beginning and end of the decoding process, prompting the model to generate a rationale and then a final answer. Experiments on various LLM benchmarks show that TORSO outperforms baseline methods, suggesting it can effectively guide models to construct their own reasoning paths. The paper also includes qualitative evaluations and ablation studies to analyze the effectiveness and impact of the proposed template.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty lies in the targeted use of strategically placed tokens during the inference phase to unlock and direct LLM's internal reasoning capabilities without the need for extensive few-shot examples or fine-tuning. While the idea of influencing LLM's generation process through token manipulation isn't entirely new (e.g., pause tokens), the specific application to reasoning and the template-based approach distinguishes this work.

**Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve the generalization and efficiency of LLM reasoning. By reducing the reliance on curated prompts, TORSO aims to address the limitations of traditional in-context learning approaches. Also, avoiding additional training data requirements and providing a method applicable to a broader range of tasks further contributes to its significance. The experimental results, while not revolutionary, demonstrate a consistent improvement over baselines across different datasets and models. This warrants the paper's claim that the introduced method helps in unlocking reasoning within LLMs and achieves reasonable performance across multiple LLM benchmarks.

**Strengths:**
*   **Simple and Elegant Approach:** The method is conceptually simple and easy to implement, which makes it readily applicable to a wide range of LLMs.
*   **Improved Generalization:** The approach reduces reliance on task-specific prompts, potentially leading to better generalization across different tasks and domains.
*   **Efficiency:** The method avoids the need for additional training data and reduces input length compared to few-shot learning, making it more efficient.
*   **Clear Experimental Setup:** The experimental setup is well-defined, and the results are presented clearly with a variety of baselines for comparison.

**Weaknesses:**
*   **Limited Theoretical Understanding:** While the paper demonstrates empirical improvements, there is limited theoretical understanding of *why* the proposed template works so well. The claim that it unlocks "internal reasoning abilities" is somewhat vague and lacks a deeper explanation.
*   **Incremental Improvement:** The performance gains are not dramatically higher than other baseline methods (e.g DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B), suggesting that TORSO offers an incremental, rather than transformative, improvement. Also, the performance of TORSO method relies heavily on the effectiveness of the underlying LLM framework.
*   **Domain Limitation:** The paper mentions in its limitations, that it is effective but limited in out-of-distribution reasoning tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper.

*   The novelty is reasonable, although the approach builds upon existing techniques in prompt engineering and token manipulation.

*   The significance lies in the practical utility of the method for improving the efficiency and generalizability of LLM reasoning and the consistent outcome across benchmarks.

*   The paper has a clear and simple approach to eliciting the model's inherent reasoning capabilities.

However, the method may not scale up to reasoning with out-of-distribution data and the lack of a strong theoretical foundation and limited improvement over the top benchmark prevents a higher score. Furthermore, the claim of unlocking internal reasoning abilities, while potentially true, lacks a rigorous explanation.
In conclusion, this paper introduces a valuable and practical method for guiding LLM reasoning, but its impact is tempered by the limitations described above.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Composable Score-based Graph Diffusion Model for Multi-Conditional Molecular Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09451v1)
- **Authors**: Anjie Qiao, Zhen Wang, Chuan Chen, DeFu Lian, Enhong Chen
- **Abstract**: Controllable molecular graph generation is essential for material and drug discovery, where generated molecules must satisfy diverse property constraints. While recent advances in graph diffusion models have improved generation quality, their effectiveness in multi-conditional settings remains limited due to reliance on joint conditioning or continuous relaxations that compromise fidelity. To address these limitations, we propose Composable Score-based Graph Diffusion model (CSGD), the first model that extends score matching to discrete graphs via concrete scores, enabling flexible and principled manipulation of conditional guidance. Building on this foundation, we introduce two score-based techniques: Composable Guidance (CoG), which allows fine-grained control over arbitrary subsets of conditions during sampling, and Probability Calibration (PC), which adjusts estimated transition probabilities to mitigate train-test mismatches. Empirical results on four molecular datasets show that CSGD achieves state-of-the-art performance, with a 15.3% average improvement in controllability over prior methods, while maintaining high validity and distributional fidelity. Our findings highlight the practical advantages of score-based modeling for discrete graph generation and its capacity for flexible, multi-property molecular design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Composable Score-based Graph Diffusion (CSGD), a novel approach for multi-conditional molecular generation.  CSGD extends score matching to discrete graphs using concrete scores, enabling flexible manipulation of conditional guidance. The paper presents two key techniques: Composable Guidance (CoG) allows fine-grained control over conditions during sampling, and Probability Calibration (PC) adjusts transition probabilities to address train-test mismatches. Experimental results on molecular datasets demonstrate that CSGD achieves state-of-the-art performance, showing improvements in controllability, validity, and distributional fidelity compared to existing methods. The authors demonstrate the practical application of score-based modeling to discrete graph generation for flexible multi-property molecular design.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in extending score-based methods, typically used in continuous domains, to discrete graph generation. The introduction of concrete scores to graph diffusion models is a significant contribution. The Composable Guidance and Probability Calibration techniques, while building upon existing concepts, are tailored effectively for molecular graph generation and provide concrete improvements in controllability.

**Significance:**

*   **Improvement in Controllability:** A 15.3% average improvement in controllability is a substantial gain, addressing a major limitation of existing graph diffusion models in multi-conditional settings.
*   **State-of-the-Art Performance:** Achieving state-of-the-art results on multiple datasets demonstrates the practical utility of the proposed method. The preservation of validity and distributional fidelity alongside improved controllability is a key strength.
*   **Flexible Molecular Design:** The composable guidance feature directly addresses the real-world need to design molecules satisfying arbitrary combinations of properties. This flexibility makes the method more useful for practical applications.
*   **Score-Based Methods in Graph Diffusion:** By successfully applying score-based methods to discrete graph generation, the paper opens avenues for further research and development in this area.
*   **Clear Ablation Studies:** The paper provides thorough ablation studies, effectively isolating the contribution of each component and clearly demonstrating the benefits of score-based modeling, composable guidance, and probability calibration.

**Weaknesses and Limitations:**

*   **Computational Cost:** While not explicitly discussed, score-based methods can be computationally intensive, particularly in high-dimensional discrete spaces. The paper should acknowledge and discuss the computational cost implications.
*   **Conditional Independence Assumption:** The composable guidance relies on the assumption of conditional independence between properties. While the authors acknowledge that this might not always hold, further investigation of the impact of correlated properties on performance is warranted.
*   **Limited Exploration of Calibration Parameters:** The Probability Calibration has 3 parameters, the tuning of which is noted as important. No information regarding parameter choice is given other than to set a and b as 1% and 99%. An analysis of the role of these parameters and guidance for their tuning should have been given.
*   **Scope of Datasets:**  While the paper uses several standard datasets, including data from specialized domains might further highlight the method's applicability to more complex real-world scenarios.

**Potential Influence:**

This work is likely to influence future research in graph generation, particularly in the context of molecular design and drug discovery. The score-based framework and the techniques for improving controllability can inspire new methods and be extended to other graph-related tasks.  The results highlight the value of flexible, multi-conditional generation methods, pushing the field towards more practical and efficient techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, this paper presents a strong and significant contribution to the field of graph generation. The core idea of extending score-based methods to discrete graph diffusion, combined with practical techniques like composable guidance and probability calibration, yields notable improvements in controllability, validity, and distribution learning. While there are some limitations related to computational cost and the independence assumption, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. A score of 8 reflects the paper's novelty, significance, and potential impact on the field, while also acknowledging the areas for further improvement and investigation.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### FlexiD-Fuse: Flexible number of inputs multi-modal medical image fusion based on diffusion model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09456v1)
- **Authors**: Yushen Xu, Xiaosong Li, Yuchun Wang, Xiaoqi Cheng, Huafeng Li, Haishu Tan
- **Abstract**: Different modalities of medical images provide unique physiological and anatomical information for diseases. Multi-modal medical image fusion integrates useful information from different complementary medical images with different modalities, producing a fused image that comprehensively and objectively reflects lesion characteristics to assist doctors in clinical diagnosis. However, existing fusion methods can only handle a fixed number of modality inputs, such as accepting only two-modal or tri-modal inputs, and cannot directly process varying input quantities, which hinders their application in clinical settings. To tackle this issue, we introduce FlexiD-Fuse, a diffusion-based image fusion network designed to accommodate flexible quantities of input modalities. It can end-to-end process two-modal and tri-modal medical image fusion under the same weight. FlexiD-Fuse transforms the diffusion fusion problem, which supports only fixed-condition inputs, into a maximum likelihood estimation problem based on the diffusion process and hierarchical Bayesian modeling. By incorporating the Expectation-Maximization algorithm into the diffusion sampling iteration process, FlexiD-Fuse can generate high-quality fused images with cross-modal information from source images, independently of the number of input images. We compared the latest two and tri-modal medical image fusion methods, tested them on Harvard datasets, and evaluated them using nine popular metrics. The experimental results show that our method achieves the best performance in medical image fusion with varying inputs. Meanwhile, we conducted extensive extension experiments on infrared-visible, multi-exposure, and multi-focus image fusion tasks with arbitrary numbers, and compared them with the perspective SOTA methods. The results of the extension experiments consistently demonstrate the effectiveness and superiority of our method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "FlexiD-Fuse," a diffusion model-based framework for multi-modal medical image fusion designed to handle a flexible number of input modalities (two, three, or theoretically more).  FlexiD-Fuse uses a hierarchical Bayesian modeling approach combined with the Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm within the diffusion process. This allows the model to adapt to varying input quantities without retraining.  The authors also propose an "ultra-lightweight diffusion fusion Mamba" (DFM) to accelerate inference.  The paper presents comparisons against state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods on medical imaging datasets, demonstrating improved performance across several metrics.  Furthermore, the authors extend FlexiD-Fuse to non-medical image fusion tasks (infrared/visible, multi-exposure, multi-focus) to demonstrate its generalizability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The key novelty lies in its ability to handle a *flexible* number of input modalities for medical image fusion *within a single trained model*.  Traditional methods are generally restricted to a fixed number of inputs (two or three), requiring separate models for different modality combinations. This significantly improves the adaptability and practical utility of the fusion method in clinical settings where modality availability can vary. The introduction of DFM using Mamba architecture for faster inference, while not entirely novel *in itself*, contributes practically to the application of diffusion models which are generally computationally expensive.  The use of Bayesian modeling and EM within a diffusion framework for image fusion is also a valuable innovation.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a practical limitation of existing medical image fusion techniques. The ability to fuse images from a variable number of modalities provides a significant benefit to clinicians, as it allows them to select the most appropriate combination of images based on the specific needs of each patient without the need to utilize a two-step process involving sequential fusing of images, or train new models.  The improved performance demonstrated across a range of metrics and datasets suggests that FlexiD-Fuse could potentially lead to better diagnostic accuracy and treatment planning. The extension to non-medical imaging tasks further broadens the potential impact.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a practical problem:** The flexible modality input is a real need in clinical practice.
*   **Strong empirical results:**  The paper provides comprehensive experimental results, comparing FlexiD-Fuse against multiple SOTA methods on various datasets and tasks. The use of several different metrics increases the confidence in the results.
*   **Well-explained method:** The paper provides a clear and detailed explanation of the FlexiD-Fuse framework, including the Bayesian modeling, EM algorithm, and DFM architecture.
*   **Generalization:** Demonstrating the performance on tasks outside the medical domain is a strength.
*   **Code availability**: Reproducibility is enhanced by the availability of the code.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational cost:** While DFM improves inference speed, the inherent computational cost of diffusion models remains a concern for real-time clinical applications.  A more thorough comparison of inference times with other lightweight methods would be valuable.
*   **Limited modality switching ability** While the models can operate on 2-3 modal images, there is no discussion on switching between the different image types beyond basic inclusion of any particular image.
*   **Incremental Innovation?** While innovative, the paper builds heavily on existing diffusion model architectures and the EM algorithm for image processing. It's more of an *integration* and *adaptation* of these existing techniques to the specific problem of medical image fusion, rather than a completely revolutionary approach.

**Potential Influence:**

FlexiD-Fuse has the potential to influence medical image analysis by offering a more adaptable and versatile fusion solution.  It could be adopted by researchers and clinicians looking to improve diagnostic accuracy and streamline workflows. The approach of combining diffusion models with Bayesian inference and EM could also inspire further research in other image processing tasks.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:** FlexiD-Fuse offers a significant improvement over existing methods by addressing a practical constraint in medical image fusion (fixed modality inputs). The strong empirical results, thorough explanation, and code availability support its potential impact. However, the relatively high computational cost and reliance on existing diffusion model techniques, coupled with incremental innovation, prevent a higher score. This paper provides a solid engineering solution to a pertinent real-world problem and is likely to be further developed by other researchers as it addresses a central limitation.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Changing the Paradigm from Dynamic Queries to LLM-generated SQL Queries with Human Intervention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09461v1)
- **Authors**: Ambre Assor, Hyeon Jeon, Sungbok Shin, Jean-Daniel Fekete
- **Abstract**: We propose leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) as an interaction layer for medical visualization systems. In domains like healthcare, where users must navigate high-dimensional, coded, and heterogeneous datasets, LLM-generated queries enable expert medical users to express complex analytical intents in natural language. These intents are then translated into editable and executable queries, replacing the dynamic query interfaces used by traditional visualization systems built around sliders, check boxes, and drop-downs. This interaction model reduces visual clutter and eliminates the need for users to memorize field names or system codes, supporting fluid exploration, with the drawback of not exposing all the filtering criteria. We also reintroduce dynamic queries on demand to better support interactive exploration. We posit that medical users are trained to know the possible filtering options but challenged to remember the details of the attribute names and code values. We demonstrate this paradigm in ParcoursVis, our scalable EventFlow-inspired patient care pathway visualization system powered by the French National Health Data System, one of the largest health data repositories in the world.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel interaction paradigm for medical visualization systems, specifically those dealing with Electronic Health Record (EHR) data.  It suggests replacing traditional dynamic query interfaces (sliders, checkboxes, dropdowns) with LLM-generated SQL queries augmented with human intervention.  The key idea is to allow medical experts to express complex analytical intents in natural language, which the LLM then translates into editable and executable SQL queries.  The authors also emphasize the importance of paired natural language explanations for each generated query to enhance transparency and user understanding. They showcase this approach within their Parcours Vis system, built on the French National Health Data System (SNDS).  The paper also discusses challenges such as the need for standardized datasets (advocating for OMOP) and the potential for integrating dynamic query interfaces alongside the LLM-based approach by extracting SQL subtrees to drive widget generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core concept of using LLMs to generate SQL queries from natural language input for medical visualization is not entirely novel.  However, the paper's emphasis on human-guided LLM query generation—where the *editability* and *explainability* of the SQL are key—provides a valuable extension.  Many existing approaches simply treat LLMs as black boxes, whereas this work prioritizes user agency and understanding. The discussion on reviving dynamic queries based on SQL subtrees is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in EHR visualization: the difficulty of navigating high-dimensional, coded datasets. Traditional dynamic query interfaces become unwieldy with a large number of attributes. LLMs offer a promising solution by enabling more natural and expressive queries. The potential impact is significant, as it could empower medical professionals to explore EHR data more effectively, leading to better insights and improved patient care. The emphasis on standardizing data formats (OMOP) is also crucial for broader applicability.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing dynamic query interfaces in the context of large EHR datasets.
    *   **Pragmatic Approach:** The human-guided LLM approach strikes a good balance between automation and user control.  It acknowledges the limitations of LLMs (e.g., hallucinations, potential inaccuracies) and incorporates mechanisms for users to inspect, edit, and understand the generated queries.
    *   **Emphasis on Explainability:** The inclusion of natural language explanations significantly enhances transparency and trust.
    *   **Relevant Use Case:** The application to the French SNDS database provides a concrete example and demonstrates the potential scalability of the approach.
    *   **Discussion of Challenges:** The paper acknowledges the challenges associated with dataset standardization and suggests practical solutions (e.g., flat table formats).
    *   **Future Directions:**  The exploration of reviving dynamic queries from SQL fragments creates a promising path towards a hybrid interaction model.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The paper lacks a rigorous evaluation with real medical users. The discussion about their proposed method is only theoretical. While demonstrating that an LLM *can* generate SQL queries is useful, it would be more impactful to show how medical users benefit from this approach compared to existing methods (e.g., in terms of task completion time, accuracy, or user satisfaction). It is unclear if the users can easily edit the SQL query generated by the LLM.
    *   **Lack of Concrete Implementation Details:** While the authors describe their vision and prototype, there are limited details about the specific implementation of the LLM integration, the query explanation module, and the SQL subtree extraction method. The performance of this LLM based method compared to traditional queries is not discussed.
    *   **LLM Selection Justification:** Although the authors argue they chose Mistral LLM as it is an open source option, there is no argument as to whether this LLM works the best compared to other LLMs.
    *   **Limited comparison to other LLM-based EHR query systems:** While the paper highlights a few papers in the related work, there are other systems that apply similar methods. More papers on how to query LLMs from an EHR could provide the reader with more clarity.

*   **Significance Score Justification:**

    The paper presents a sound concept with clear potential benefits, but it falls short in terms of evaluation and implementation detail. The focus on human-guided query generation and explainability is valuable and differentiates it from simpler LLM-based approaches. However, the lack of user studies and detailed implementation information limits the paper's impact. It lacks in comparison and discussion of other similar works. While the idea is sound, it needs additional research and is not fully developed.
