### Database Views as Explanations for Relational Deep Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09482v1)
- **Authors**: Agapi Rissaki, Ilias Fountalis, Wolfgang Gatterbauer, Benny Kimelfeld
- **Abstract**: In recent years, there has been significant progress in the development of deep learning models over relational databases, including architectures based on heterogeneous graph neural networks (hetero-GNNs) and heterogeneous graph transformers. In effect, such architectures state how the database records and links (e.g., foreign-key references) translate into a large, complex numerical expression, involving numerous learnable parameters. This complexity makes it hard to explain, in human-understandable terms, how a model uses the available data to arrive at a given prediction. We present a novel framework for explaining machine-learning models over relational databases, where explanations are view definitions that highlight focused parts of the database that mostly contribute to the model's prediction. We establish such global abductive explanations by adapting the classic notion of determinacy by Nash, Segoufin, and Vianu (2010). In addition to tuning the tradeoff between determinacy and conciseness, the framework allows controlling the level of granularity by adopting different fragments of view definitions, such as ones highlighting whole columns, foreign keys between tables, relevant groups of tuples, and so on. We investigate the realization of the framework in the case of hetero-GNNs. We develop heuristic algorithms that avoid the exhaustive search over the space of all databases. We propose techniques that are model-agnostic, and others that are tailored to hetero-GNNs via the notion of learnable masking. Our approach is evaluated through an extensive empirical study on the RelBench collection, covering a variety of domains and different record-level tasks. The results demonstrate the usefulness of the proposed explanations, as well as the efficiency of their generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Database Views as Explanations for Relational Deep Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel framework for explaining the behavior of deep learning models trained on relational databases, particularly those using heterogeneous graph neural networks (hetero-GNNs). The explanations take the form of SQL view definitions that highlight the portions of the database most relevant to the model's predictions. The core idea is based on a statistical adaptation of the classical concept of determinacy, where a view is considered a good explanation if perturbing the database outside the view has minimal impact on the model's output. The framework supports user-controlled granularity in the explanation views (e.g., column projections, foreign-key joins, selections), and it includes heuristic algorithms tailored for hetero-GNNs using learned masking. The authors evaluate their approach empirically on the RelBench benchmark, demonstrating its effectiveness in providing concise and informative explanations while maintaining prediction accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using database views as explanations for relational deep learning models is genuinely novel.  It addresses the challenge of explaining complex numerical functions induced by GNNs in a way that is directly interpretable by database users, who are often more familiar with SQL than with GNN internals. The statistical adaptation of determinacy to this context is a solid theoretical foundation.
*   **Significance:** This work tackles a critical problem: the lack of explainability in deep learning models applied to relational data. If relational data is a core asset in many organizations, tools to understand why a deep learning model trained on it is predicting it a certain way is crucial. Improving interpretability can foster trust, improve model debugging, and ensure fairness.  The choice to ground the explanations in SQL directly increases the practicality of the approach and the chance of adoption. The fact that these explanations could also improve model efficiency is also an added bonus.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper articulates the problem clearly, providing a compelling motivation for explainable AI in the context of relational deep learning.
    *   **Sound Theoretical Foundation:** The adaptation of determinacy provides a formal basis for evaluating the quality of explanations.
    *   **Practical Approach:** The use of SQL view definitions makes the explanations more accessible to database practitioners.  The masking-based heuristics offer a practical way to generate explanations without exhaustively searching the database space.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The empirical evaluation on the RelBench benchmark is thorough, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach across diverse datasets and tasks.
    *   **Code Availability:** Open-sourcing the code promotes reproducibility and facilitates further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Heuristic Nature:** The implementation relies on heuristics, particularly the masking-based approach. While effective in practice, it lacks theoretical guarantees of optimality. There's no clear way to know how far the heuristic solution is from the "best" explanation in terms of determinacy.
    *   **Limited Explanation Language:** The focus on projections, joins, and selections is reasonable, but may not capture all relevant explanation patterns. The framework could benefit from extending to more expressive SQL constructs.
    *   **Instance-agnostic Focus:** The reliance on instance-agnostic explanations simplifies implementation, but may miss insights specific to particular predictions. Instance-level explanations offer complementary insights, though are indeed more difficult to discover.
    *   **Lack of Comparison with Direct GNN Explanation Methods:**  While SQL is beneficial for DBAs, it would be useful to compare the SQL view explanation approach to GNN explanation methods that work directly on the graph structure to help practitioners decide which is more useful.
    *   **Trade-offs in Explanation Size:**  The paper acknowledges the trade-off between conciseness and soft determinacy.  However, it would be beneficial to provide more guidance on how to choose the optimal explanation size for different use cases.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to significantly influence the field of relational deep learning by providing a practical and interpretable way to understand model behavior. It opens new avenues for research on explanation techniques that are tailored to relational data and that leverage the power of database query languages.

**Justification for Score:**

This is a well-executed piece of research that addresses an important and timely problem. It introduces a genuinely novel approach with a solid theoretical foundation and a practical implementation. The extensive empirical evaluation provides compelling evidence of its effectiveness. While the heuristic nature of the implementation and the limitations of the explanation language represent areas for improvement, the paper's strengths significantly outweigh its weaknesses. It has the potential to drive new research directions and to have a practical impact on how deep learning models are deployed and used in relational database contexts.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 8/10

### Prompt Pirates Need a Map: Stealing Seeds helps Stealing Prompts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09488v1)
- **Authors**: Felix Mächtle, Ashwath Shetty, Jonas Sander, Nils Loose, Sören Pirk, Thomas Eisenbarth
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have significantly advanced text-to-image generation, enabling the creation of highly realistic images conditioned on textual prompts and seeds. Given the considerable intellectual and economic value embedded in such prompts, prompt theft poses a critical security and privacy concern. In this paper, we investigate prompt-stealing attacks targeting diffusion models. We reveal that numerical optimization-based prompt recovery methods are fundamentally limited as they do not account for the initial random noise used during image generation. We identify and exploit a noise-generation vulnerability (CWE-339), prevalent in major image-generation frameworks, originating from PyTorch's restriction of seed values to a range of $2^{32}$ when generating the initial random noise on CPUs. Through a large-scale empirical analysis conducted on images shared via the popular platform CivitAI, we demonstrate that approximately 95% of these images' seed values can be effectively brute-forced in 140 minutes per seed using our seed-recovery tool, SeedSnitch. Leveraging the recovered seed, we propose PromptPirate, a genetic algorithm-based optimization method explicitly designed for prompt stealing. PromptPirate surpasses state-of-the-art methods, i.e., PromptStealer, P2HP, and CLIP-Interrogator, achieving an 8-11% improvement in LPIPS similarity. Furthermore, we introduce straightforward and effective countermeasures that render seed stealing, and thus optimization-based prompt stealing, ineffective. We have disclosed our findings responsibly and initiated coordinated mitigation efforts with the developers to address this critical vulnerability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Prompt Pirates Need a Map: Stealing Seeds helps Stealing Prompts":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the security vulnerability of prompt stealing in diffusion models used for text-to-image generation. It argues that existing prompt recovery methods are fundamentally limited because they ignore the initial random noise (seed) used in the generation process. The authors identify a common vulnerability (CWE-339) across popular image generation frameworks: the limited seed range due to PyTorch's CPU-based seed truncation. They then introduce SeedSnitch, a tool to efficiently brute-force these limited seed values from shared images.  Using the recovered seed, they propose PromptPirate, a genetic algorithm-based optimization method for prompt stealing that leverages knowledge of the initial noise. The authors demonstrate that PromptPirate outperforms existing prompt stealing techniques and highlight the importance of seed security.  They also suggest straightforward countermeasures and advocate for responsible disclosure.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper makes a significant contribution by identifying and exploiting the role of the initial seed in prompt stealing. While prompt stealing itself isn't a new problem, the focus on the initial noise and how weaknesses in its generation (and the ability to recover it) exacerbate the problem is novel. The use of a genetic algorithm tailored to this specific problem also has some novelty. The identification and responsible disclosure of the CWE-339 vulnerability is also a valuable contribution.
* **Significance:** The paper highlights a significant security concern with economic and intellectual property implications. As text-to-image models are increasingly used for commercial applications, the potential for prompt theft and the ease with which it can be accomplished if the initial noise is not properly secured raises serious questions about the current state of deployment and development. The proposed countermeasures offer a practical solution for addressing the identified vulnerabilities. The empirical analysis based on real-world data (CivitAI) adds to the significance by showing that the vulnerability is not merely theoretical.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the prompt stealing problem and explains why existing methods are insufficient.
    *   **Practical Attack:** SeedSnitch offers a very practical and efficient seed recovery technique, which is the cornerstone of their prompt stealing method.
    *   **Strong Empirical Evaluation:**  The experiments are well-designed and the results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of SeedSnitch and PromptPirate.
    *   **Responsible Disclosure:** The authors took a responsible approach by disclosing their findings to relevant developers and obtaining CVEs.
    *   **Practical Recommendations:**  The proposed countermeasures are relatively simple to implement.
* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Vulnerability:** The attack heavily relies on the seed recovery, which in turn relies on the specific weakness (CWE-339) they identified. While the weakness is prevalent in several popular tools, it may not be a long-term vulnerability as developers will likely address it.
    *  **Scalability:** While their genetic algorithm performs well, genetic algorithms are known to struggle scaling to significantly larger search spaces. The increase in performance, while significant, is limited at around 8-11% improvement.
    * **Lack of alternative to brute-force:** A more sophisticated seed recovery method that is not brute-force based would have been a better contribution, especially now that these seeds are likely to be better protected in newer implementations.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings should influence the development and deployment of text-to-image models. Developers should adopt more secure random number generation practices and consider the privacy implications of sharing images with their associated metadata. This will potentially lead to a wave of improvements in how seeds are managed and used in AI image generation frameworks. The introduction of the genetic algorithm could lead to other improvements in how prompts are optimized, even without knowledge of seeds.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7.  The paper presents a novel and significant security vulnerability in a very hot topic: text-to-image generation.  The combination of SeedSnitch and PromptPirate demonstrates a real-world attack that highlights a critical weakness. The experiments are well-executed and the paper provides practical recommendations. However, the over-reliance on a specific, and potentially short-lived, vulnerability (CWE-339) limits the lasting impact. The dependence of brute-force on SeedSnitch is also seen as a shortcoming. Nevertheless, the paper's contribution to understanding prompt security in diffusion models is substantial.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mixture of Semantics Transmission for Generative AI-Enabled Semantic Communication Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09499v1)
- **Authors**: Junjie Ni, Tong Wu, Zhiyong Chen, Yin Xu, Meixia Tao, Wenjun Zhang
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose a mixture of semantics (MoS) transmission strategy for wireless semantic communication systems based on generative artificial intelligence (AI). At the transmitter, we divide an image into regions of interest (ROI) and reigons of non-interest (RONI) to extract their semantic information respectively. Semantic information of ROI can be allocated more bandwidth, while RONI can be represented in a compact form for transmission. At the receiver, a diffusion model reconstructs the full image using the received semantic information of ROI and RONI. Compared to existing generative AI-based methods, MoS enables more efficient use of channel resources by balancing visual fidelity and semantic relevance. Experimental results demonstrate that appropriate ROI-RONI allocation is critical. The MoS achieves notable performance gains in peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) of ROI and CLIP score of RONI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a "Mixture of Semantics" (MoS) transmission strategy for wireless semantic communication systems. The core idea is to leverage generative AI to intelligently split an image into regions of interest (ROI) and regions of non-interest (RONI). The ROI is transmitted using more bandwidth via a standard image JSCC encoder, while the RONI is converted into a text description using a vision-language model (BLIP) and transmitted using a text JSCC encoder. At the receiver, a diffusion model reconstructs the full image using the received ROI data and the semantic information derived from the RONI text description. The authors demonstrate through experiments that this approach can balance visual fidelity and semantic relevance, leading to more efficient use of channel resources compared to existing methods. They use PSNR for ROI quality and CLIP score for RONI semantic similarity as evaluation metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The idea of splitting an image into ROI and RONI and transmitting them with different semantic modalities *is* a reasonably novel approach within the field of wireless semantic communications, particularly in the context of generative AI.  The use of SAM to automatically identify these regions and the use of different encoders/decoders for each region are well motivated. However, the individual components (SAM, BLIP, Diffusion Models, JSCC) are not novel *per se*. The primary novelty lies in *how* these existing components are integrated and the MoS strategy itself. It addresses a practical trade-off between pixel-level accuracy and semantic relevance. The paper attempts to innovate a strategy which allocates different computational resources based on relevance, it however doesn't innovate on existing architectures.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential for more efficient use of limited bandwidth in wireless communication. By transmitting the RONI as text, the bandwidth requirements are significantly reduced, and the diffusion model can plausibly reconstruct it, leveraging context from the ROI. Demonstrating gains in PSNR and CLIP score supports this potential. The paper directly addresses the resource constraints common in wireless environments and potentially improves the quality of reconstructed images within those constraints. The improvement in CLIP-RONI scores is significant when compared to the baseline.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of efficiently transmitting images over wireless channels while preserving both visual fidelity and semantic information.
    * **Clear Methodology:** The MoS strategy is well-explained, with clear descriptions of the components and their roles.
    * **Experimental Validation:** The use of standard datasets (RefCOCO, Pascal VOC) and established metrics (PSNR, CLIP Score, BLEU) provides a solid basis for evaluating the performance of the proposed approach. The comparative study with other state-of-the-art models offers good insight.
    * **Parameter Study:** Examining the impact of the `alpha` parameter (the proportion of resources allocated to text) provides valuable insights into the system's behavior and highlights the importance of balancing the transmission ratio between ROI and RONI.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Innovation in Core Components:** The paper relies heavily on existing AI models (SAM, BLIP, Diffusion Models).  While the integration is novel, the paper doesn't introduce substantial modifications or improvements to these core components.
    * **Complexity:** The approach significantly increases system complexity due to the integration of various large AI models. The high FLOPS and parameter count are potentially a limitation in resource-constrained devices, and should be examined with more in-depth analysis
    * **Generalization:** The experiments are primarily focused on specific datasets (RefCOCO, Pascal VOC). It would be beneficial to see results on other types of images or data with different characteristics to assess the generalizability of the approach. The dataset could also include some robustness datasets to see how the model performs under more realistic situations, i.e. weather interference.
    * **Visual Results:** The visual results shown are not particularly convincing, and the advantages of MoS are not immediately obvious from the figures. The images are relatively small, and a more detailed analysis of the reconstructed image quality would be beneficial. The images should include the segmentation map as well for increased clarity.
* **Rayleigh Fading Model:** Should the Rayleigh Fading model be applicable? In what scenario is it used. It may be more applicable to a different scenario.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6.5/10**.

*   The novelty is decent but not groundbreaking, as it primarily combines existing AI models in a novel way.
*   The significance is moderate, as the approach addresses a practical problem in wireless communication. The gains in performance are promising, but the real-world applicability needs further investigation considering its complexity.
*   The weaknesses, particularly the reliance on pre-trained models and the lack of innovation in the core components, limit the score. The practical implications of the higher complexity also raise concerns. Further research is needed to assess the wider applicability and robustness of the MoS strategy. Overall, it represents a reasonable contribution with room for significant improvement. The results showcase gains within experimental conditions but needs to demonstrate gains in more real-world scenarios.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.IT
- **Score**: 6/10

### DeMeVa at LeWiDi-2025: Modeling Perspectives with In-Context Learning and Label Distribution Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09524v1)
- **Authors**: Daniil Ignatev, Nan Li, Hugh Mee Wong, Anh Dang, Shane Kaszefski Yaschuk
- **Abstract**: This system paper presents the DeMeVa team's approaches to the third edition of the Learning with Disagreements shared task (LeWiDi 2025; Leonardelli et al., 2025). We explore two directions: in-context learning (ICL) with large language models, where we compare example sampling strategies; and label distribution learning (LDL) methods with RoBERTa (Liu et al., 2019b), where we evaluate several fine-tuning methods. Our contributions are twofold: (1) we show that ICL can effectively predict annotator-specific annotations (perspectivist annotations), and that aggregating these predictions into soft labels yields competitive performance; and (2) we argue that LDL methods are promising for soft label predictions and merit further exploration by the perspectivist community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper describes the DeMeVa team's approach to the Learning with Disagreements (LeWiDi) 2025 shared task. The team explored two primary methods: in-context learning (ICL) using large language models (LLMs) and label distribution learning (LDL) with RoBERTa. For ICL, they compared different example sampling strategies (similarity-based and stratified label-based). For LDL, they evaluated ordinal label distribution learning and predicting population-level label distributions via clustering.  The team found that ICL can effectively predict annotator-specific annotations, and aggregating these predictions yields competitive performance. They also suggest that LDL methods are promising for soft label predictions and should be further explored by the perspectivist community. Their ICL approach achieved second place in the LeWiDi competition.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty is somewhat limited. Applying ICL to perspectivist tasks and comparing different prompting/sampling strategies is not entirely new, although the specific application to the LeWiDi datasets and the comparison of similarity-based vs. stratified label-based sampling provides some incremental advancement. The exploration of LDL methods, particularly ordinal LDL and population-level distributions, within the NLP perspectivist learning space is a slightly more novel contribution. These LDL methods haven't seen widespread adoption within NLP despite existing in broader machine learning.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its empirical evaluation of ICL and LDL methods on the LeWiDi datasets. The finding that ICL can effectively predict annotator-specific annotations is valuable, as it confirms the potential of LLMs for modeling subjective judgments. Similarly, the exploration of LDL methods suggests an alternative approach for learning from disagreement that deserves further investigation. The paper's analysis of failure cases, such as the model's tendency to predict unanimous agreement even when annotators disagree, highlights challenges in achieving true pluralistic alignment. These analyses and insights contribute to a better understanding of learning from disagreements. A key contribution is also highlighting the benefit of explanation data when using ICL.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear description of the methodologies and experiments.
    *   Thorough analysis of the results, including both successes and failures.
    *   Exploration of different techniques (ICL and LDL) for the same problem.
    *   Discussion of the potential benefits of LDL for the perspectivist community.
    *   Highlighting the ethical considerations associated with using personalized annotations.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The results of ICL methods are not overwhelmingly conclusive, with only incremental improvements.
    *   The exploration of LDL methods is limited to a subset of the Task A.
    *   The datasets used in the shared task may be relatively small (especially the development sets), which may limit the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The novelty is incremental, mainly applying existing techniques to a new task.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's potential influence is moderate. It contributes to the growing body of research on ICL and learning from disagreement, providing valuable empirical evidence and insights. The exploration of LDL methods may inspire further research in this area. The paper's discussion of ethical considerations is also important, as it raises awareness of the potential risks associated with using personalized annotations.

**Justification:**

The paper successfully applies existing techniques, particularly ICL, to the LeWiDi dataset, achieving a good performance result. The exploration of LDL methods within the NLP perspectivist learning community is also valuable, although the empirical results are less conclusive. While the paper doesn't introduce groundbreaking techniques or address a completely new problem, it provides a solid empirical evaluation and insightful analysis that contribute to the field. The limitations of the datasets and the incremental nature of the novelty justify a lower score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Prompting the Market? A Large-Scale Meta-Analysis of GenAI in Finance NLP (2022-2025)
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09544v1)
- **Authors**: Paolo Pedinotti, Peter Baumann, Nathan Jessurun, Leslie Barrett, Enrico Santus
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have rapidly reshaped financial NLP, enabling new tasks and driving a proliferation of datasets and diversification of data sources. Yet, this transformation has outpaced traditional surveys. In this paper, we present MetaGraph, a generalizable methodology for extracting knowledge graphs from scientific literature and analyzing them to obtain a structured, queryable view of research trends. We define an ontology for financial NLP research and apply an LLM-based extraction pipeline to 681 papers (2022-2025), enabling large-scale, data-driven analysis. MetaGraph reveals three key phases: early LLM adoption and task/dataset innovation; critical reflection on LLM limitations; and growing integration of peripheral techniques into modular systems. This structured view offers both practitioners and researchers a clear understanding of how financial NLP has evolved - highlighting emerging trends, shifting priorities, and methodological shifts-while also demonstrating a reusable approach for mapping scientific progress in other domains.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Prompting the Market? A Large-Scale Meta-Analysis of GenAI in Finance NLP (2022-2025)" introduces MetaGraph, a novel methodology for automated knowledge graph construction from scientific literature. It aims to provide a structured, queryable view of research trends in Financial NLP, particularly regarding the impact of Generative AI (GenAI). The authors define an ontology for financial NLP research and apply an LLM-based extraction pipeline to 681 papers published between 2022 and 2025. The analysis reveals three key phases: early LLM adoption and task/dataset innovation, critical reflection on LLM limitations, and growing integration of peripheral techniques into modular systems. The paper offers practitioners and researchers a clear understanding of how financial NLP has evolved, highlighting emerging trends and methodological shifts, while also demonstrating a reusable approach for mapping scientific progress in other domains. The resulting knowledge graph is made available for reuse.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Methodology:** The introduction of MetaGraph is a significant strength. Using knowledge graphs to analyze research trends provides a more structured and quantitative approach than traditional literature surveys. The method is claimed to be generalizable and reusable across other domains.
*   **Quantitative Analysis:** The paper's ability to extract quantitative insights from a large corpus of research papers is valuable.  It provides a data-driven perspective on the evolution of the field, supporting claims with evidence from the extracted knowledge graph.
*   **Timeliness:** The paper addresses a rapidly evolving field where traditional surveys struggle to keep pace. It offers a current snapshot (2022-2025) of GenAI's impact on Financial NLP.
*   **Practical Insights:** The identified phases of LLM adoption, reflection on limitations, and system-level integration provide valuable insights for both practitioners and researchers navigating this changing landscape.
*   **Open Resources:** Releasing the constructed knowledge graph is commendable, enabling other researchers to validate the findings and conduct further analysis.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **LLM-Reliance:** The core of the methodology relies heavily on LLMs for information extraction. While the authors acknowledge this as a potential limitation, the accuracy and reliability of the knowledge graph are directly tied to the LLM's performance. Errors or biases in the LLM could propagate through the analysis. While they used a 'human-in-the-loop' approach, the extent and nature of that loop isn't entirely clear. How the sampling for validation was performed might alter conclusions drawn.
*   **Ontology Bias:** The manual definition of the ontology introduces a subjective bias. While the authors aim to capture key aspects of the field, the chosen categories and relationships might influence the resulting analysis and could miss emergent themes. The details of the ontology construction process are not thoroughly elaborated.
*   **Limited Depth of Analysis:** While the paper presents a high-level overview of trends, the analysis could be deepened by exploring specific research areas or tasks in more detail. For example, specific challenges or successful integration strategies related to RAG applications in finance could be highlighted further. The analysis can be expanded by including the geographic representation and its correlation with research foci.
*   **Metrics for Evaluation:** The metrics used for evaluating the performance of the models is absent and can be strengthened with its inclusion.
*   **Missing link to the dataset:** The link to the publicly available dataset doesn't point to the right location.

**Significance:**

The paper is significant because it presents a valuable methodological approach for analyzing scientific literature in a rapidly evolving field. The insights gained from the MetaGraph analysis provide a useful overview of the impact of GenAI on Financial NLP. The reusable nature of the methodology makes it applicable to other domains experiencing rapid change.

**Novelty:**

The key novelty lies in the combination of knowledge graph construction with LLMs to analyze scientific literature at scale. While LLMs have been used for knowledge graph construction previously, this paper demonstrates its application for analyzing trends in a specific research domain.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper's methodological novelty, data-driven analysis, and timely insights justify a score of **7.5/10**. While the reliance on LLMs and the potential for ontology bias are valid concerns, the strengths of the approach and the valuable contributions it makes to understanding the evolution of Financial NLP outweigh these weaknesses. Further refinement of the methodology, deeper analysis, and more detailed explanation of the ontology construction process could elevate the score in future work.

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving Video Diffusion Transformer Training by Multi-Feature Fusion and Alignment from Self-Supervised Vision Encoders
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09547v1)
- **Authors**: Dohun Lee, Hyeonho Jeong, Jiwook Kim, Duygu Ceylan, Jong Chul Ye
- **Abstract**: Video diffusion models have advanced rapidly in the recent years as a result of series of architectural innovations (e.g., diffusion transformers) and use of novel training objectives (e.g., flow matching). In contrast, less attention has been paid to improving the feature representation power of such models. In this work, we show that training video diffusion models can benefit from aligning the intermediate features of the video generator with feature representations of pre-trained vision encoders. We propose a new metric and conduct an in-depth analysis of various vision encoders to evaluate their discriminability and temporal consistency, thereby assessing their suitability for video feature alignment. Based on the analysis, we present Align4Gen which provides a novel multi-feature fusion and alignment method integrated into video diffusion model training. We evaluate Align4Gen both for unconditional and class-conditional video generation tasks and show that it results in improved video generation as quantified by various metrics. Full video results are available on our project page: https://align4gen.github.io/align4gen/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Improving Video Diffusion Transformer Training by Multi-Feature Fusion and Alignment from Self-Supervised Vision Encoders" introduces Align4Gen, a novel framework for enhancing video diffusion model training. It leverages pre-trained self-supervised vision encoders (like DINOv2 and SAM2.1 Hiera) to guide the feature learning of video diffusion transformers (V-DiTs). The core idea is to align the intermediate features of the V-DiT with features from the pre-trained encoders.  A key contribution is a new metric, Intra-Inter Consistency Ratio (IICR), to evaluate the discriminability and temporal consistency of features from different vision encoders. The paper shows that image-trained encoders can be more suitable than video-trained encoders in some cases. Align4Gen fuses features from multiple encoders to provide a richer supervisory signal and demonstrates improved video generation quality and faster convergence on various datasets, including UCF-101, SkyTimelapse, and FaceForensics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using pre-trained visual encoders to guide diffusion model training (as seen in REPA) isn't entirely new. However, the paper introduces several novel contributions that extend this concept: 1) the IICR metric for assessing vision encoder suitability for video, considering both discriminability and temporal consistency, 2) the multi-feature fusion approach to combine complementary image encoder representations (especially low and high frequencies captured by DINOv2 and SAM2.1), and 3) a comprehensive evaluation of various encoders and their suitability as guidance for video diffusion. The combination of these elements strengthens the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important issue in video diffusion models: improving feature representation power. The results demonstrate that Align4Gen leads to tangible improvements in video generation quality (FVD, FID, Inception Score) and training efficiency. The finding that image-trained encoders can be more suitable than video-trained encoders for certain video generation tasks is also a significant contribution, providing a counterintuitive insight for the field. The faster convergence is particularly valuable, reducing the computational cost of training high-quality video models. The application of the method on different tasks, like faceforensics, shows its applicability to various types of datasets.
*   **Strengths:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and experimental setup. The introduction of IICR is a major strength, enabling a more principled approach to selecting vision encoders. The multi-feature fusion approach is well-motivated and empirically validated. The experimental results are extensive and demonstrate consistent improvements across different datasets and evaluation metrics. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the design choices of Align4Gen.
*   **Weaknesses:** While the IICR is a valuable contribution, it's important to note that the success of alignment still depends on the architecture of vision encoders used and the loss function employed for aligning features. This could be seen as a limitation of the technique. Also, the text-to-video experiments section reports difficulties, and disruption of pre-learned weights in the spatial layers of the transformer blocks.

**Overall Impact and Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of video diffusion models. The IICR metric offers a new way to evaluate vision encoders, and Align4Gen effectively leverages pre-trained encoders to improve video generation quality and training efficiency. While the core idea of feature alignment isn't entirely new, the specific implementation and the insights it provides are novel and impactful. The paper shows that image-trained encoders (DINOv2, SAM2.1) perform well (sometimes, better than other encoders). This conclusion seems sound, but the performance of this combination may not be generalizable to other types of datasets or tasks. Also, text-to-video experiments are problematic and more difficult to evaluate and interpret.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Finite Scalar Quantization Enables Redundant and Transmission-Robust Neural Audio Compression at Low Bit-rates
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09550v1)
- **Authors**: Harry Julia, Rachel Beeson, Lohith Konathala, Johanna Ulin, Jiameng Gao
- **Abstract**: Neural Audio Codecs (NACs) have become increasingly adopted in speech processing tasks due to their excellent rate-distortion performance and compatibility with Large Language Models (LLMs) as discrete feature representations for audio generation. While most existing codecs rely on Residual Vector Quantization (RVQ), Finite Scalar Quantization (FSQ) has recently emerged as a compelling alternative that simplifies training and natively supports single codebooks. We introduce NeuCodec, an FSQ-based NAC, and show that FSQ encodes baked-in redundancy which produces an encoding which is robust when transmitted through noisy channels. First, through an encoder distillation experiment, we show that two different encoders can learn to encode identical audio into vastly different code sequences whilst maintaining comparable reconstruction quality with the same quantizer and decoder. Second, we demonstrate that FSQ has vastly superior bit-level perturbation robustness by comparing the performance of RVQ and FSQ codecs when simulating the transmission of code sequences through a noisy channel.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NeuCodec, a neural audio codec (NAC) based on Finite Scalar Quantization (FSQ).  The authors argue that FSQ-based NACs offer an inherent robustness to transmission noise compared to Residual Vector Quantization (RVQ) based codecs.  They demonstrate this through an encoder distillation experiment showing different encoders can learn distinct code sequences for the same audio while maintaining reconstruction quality, and through a bit-level perturbation experiment simulating noisy channel transmission. The key finding is that FSQ codecs maintain intelligibility (STOI) better than RVQ codecs under significant bit-flipping probabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in demonstrating the noise-robustness of FSQ-based NACs.  While FSQ itself isn't new (it is cited by Mentzer, Minnen et. al, 2024), its application and observed robustness in noisy channel scenarios are a valuable contribution. The encoder distillation experiment, while conceptually interesting, primarily serves to highlight the built-in redundancy of the FSQ representation, a characteristic the authors leverage for noise robustness.
The novelty is not ground-breaking, however, it is a valuable finding that is worth pursuing in future work.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance is in enabling more reliable audio communication in scenarios with unreliable transmission channels (e.g., low bandwidth connections, wireless communication). If FSQ-based codecs can provide comparable quality to RVQ at similar bitrates *and* superior robustness, they could become attractive alternatives. The implication for low-resource scenarios or communication in challenging environments is clear.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow. The experiments are clearly defined, and the results are presented in a readily understandable format.
    *   **Empirical validation:**  The paper provides empirical evidence through both the encoder distillation and the bit-level perturbation experiments. The comparison to RVQ-based codecs is crucial for establishing the relative advantage of FSQ.
    *   **Practical relevance:**  The research has practical implications for real-world audio communication systems.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The description of NeuCodec's architecture and training details appears sufficient for replication (though code availability would strengthen this further).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Analysis:** The paper doesn't delve deeply into *why* FSQ offers superior robustness beyond the hypothesis of built-in redundancy and locality.  A more detailed analysis of the codebook structure and the impact of bit flips on the reconstructed waveform would be beneficial. Understanding the underlying mechanisms would enable better design and optimization of FSQ codecs for noise resilience.
    *   **Performance Comparison:** The performance metrics lack in terms of speech quality. While intelligibility and reconstruction quality are compared, a subjective evaluation is missing and would make the paper more impactful.
    *   **Limited Codec Comparison:**  While the paper compares to RVQ based codecs, more nuanced comparisons exploring different types of quantization, or other strategies used in robust compression, would make the results more convincing.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the design of future audio codecs, particularly for applications where transmission robustness is paramount. It could also inspire further research into exploiting redundancy in discrete representations for enhanced resilience.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by highlighting the noise-robustness of FSQ-based NACs. The experimental results provide evidence of this benefit, and the discussion offers insights into potential explanations. However, the depth of analysis could be improved. Additionally, the novelty could be improved if more codecs and performance metrics were compared to assess the advantages and disadvantages of FSQ over other existing codecs.

### Fluent but Unfeeling: The Emotional Blind Spots of Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09593v1)
- **Authors**: Bangzhao Shu, Isha Joshi, Melissa Karnaze, Anh C. Pham, Ishita Kakkar, Sindhu Kothe, Arpine Hovasapian, Mai ElSherief
- **Abstract**: The versatility of Large Language Models (LLMs) in natural language understanding has made them increasingly popular in mental health research. While many studies explore LLMs' capabilities in emotion recognition, a critical gap remains in evaluating whether LLMs align with human emotions at a fine-grained level. Existing research typically focuses on classifying emotions into predefined, limited categories, overlooking more nuanced expressions. To address this gap, we introduce EXPRESS, a benchmark dataset curated from Reddit communities featuring 251 fine-grained, self-disclosed emotion labels. Our comprehensive evaluation framework examines predicted emotion terms and decomposes them into eight basic emotions using established emotion theories, enabling a fine-grained comparison. Systematic testing of prevalent LLMs under various prompt settings reveals that accurately predicting emotions that align with human self-disclosed emotions remains challenging. Qualitative analysis further shows that while certain LLMs generate emotion terms consistent with established emotion theories and definitions, they sometimes fail to capture contextual cues as effectively as human self-disclosures. These findings highlight the limitations of LLMs in fine-grained emotion alignment and offer insights for future research aimed at enhancing their contextual understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper, "Fluent but Unfeeling: The Emotional Blind Spots of Language Models," addresses the limitations of Large Language Models (LLMs) in fine-grained emotion recognition. It introduces EXPRESS, a new benchmark dataset consisting of self-disclosed emotions from Reddit communities, offering a more nuanced and ecologically valid alternative to existing emotion datasets. The authors evaluate several LLMs using a multi-faceted framework that includes lexical accuracy, decomposed emotion vector accuracy (based on established emotion theories), and contextual accuracy (assessed through human evaluation). The results reveal that while LLMs can generate emotion terms consistent with theory, they often struggle to align with human self-disclosed emotions at a fine-grained level and may miss contextual cues.  The study explores the influence of model size, architecture, and prompting strategies, finding that masked language models and certain larger models perform better. Surprisingly, Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting degrades performance in this socially sensitive task.  The paper also finds that LLMs can learn from few-shot examples and concludes that while challenges remain, LLMs have potential for improved emotion recognition, especially in mental health applications, given targeted design and training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:

    *   **Dataset:** The construction of EXPRESS, using self-disclosed emotions from a real-world source (Reddit), addresses a key limitation of existing benchmarks that often rely on crowdsourced or expert annotations.  This approach contributes to the ecological validity of the research. The dataset contains a large number of fine-grained labels.
    *   **Evaluation Framework:** The framework that goes beyond simple lexical matching to incorporate decomposed emotion vectors based on established emotion theories (Plutchik's wheel) and contextual accuracy adds to the rigor of the evaluation. The multi-faceted metrics that encompass lexical accuracy, decomposed emotion vector accuracy, and contextual accuracy
    *   **Prompting Strategies:** The investigation of different prompting strategies (zero-shot, few-shot, CoT) is valuable, revealing that CoT is not always beneficial and may even hinder performance in socially sensitive tasks. This contributes to a more nuanced understanding of how to effectively use LLMs for emotion-related tasks.
    *   **Human Evaluation:** Incorporating expert human evaluation to measure "contextual accuracy" provides a qualitative perspective often missing in LLM evaluation.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant for several reasons:

    *   **Addressing a Critical Gap:** The paper highlights a critical gap in current research on LLMs and emotion recognition, which often focuses on classifying emotions into limited categories, overlooking fine-grained nuances.
    *   **Informing Future Research:** The paper's findings offer valuable insights for future research aimed at enhancing LLMs' contextual understanding and emotion alignment. The identified challenges and limitations can guide the development of more effective training strategies and evaluation metrics.
    *   **Implications for Applications:** The work has implications for the development of emotion-aware AI systems, especially in sensitive domains like mental health, where accurate and nuanced emotion recognition is crucial for building trust and providing appropriate support.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a rigorous methodology, including a well-defined evaluation framework, systematic testing of several LLMs, and qualitative analysis.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents its research questions, methods, results, and conclusions.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper provides a comprehensive analysis of the factors that influence LLM performance in emotion recognition, including model size, architecture, and prompting strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset limitations:** While Reddit offers ecological validity, its demographics and content may not be fully representative of all populations. This may limit the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Human Evaluation limitations:** The low inter-coder agreement in the human evaluation portion suggest that determining "contextual accuracy" may be a subjective and challenging task.
    *   **Lack of Task-Specific Fine-Tuning:** The paper primarily evaluates pre-trained LLMs without task-specific fine-tuning. Fine-tuning on the EXPRESS dataset could potentially improve performance and provide a more accurate assessment of the models' capabilities.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

    *   **Encouraging the Development of More Ecologically Valid Benchmarks:** The EXPRESS dataset can serve as a valuable resource for evaluating LLMs' emotion recognition capabilities and promoting the development of more realistic benchmarks.
    *   **Guiding the Design of More Effective Training Strategies:** The findings on the limitations of CoT prompting and the benefits of few-shot learning can inform the design of more effective training strategies for LLMs in emotion-related tasks.
    *   **Promoting Interdisciplinary Collaboration:** The paper's interdisciplinary approach, combining techniques from NLP, psychology, and affective science, can encourage further collaboration between researchers from different fields.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper makes a significant contribution by addressing a critical gap in LLM research and providing a more nuanced and ecologically valid approach to evaluating emotion recognition. The construction of the EXPRESS dataset, the multi-faceted evaluation framework, and the comprehensive analysis of factors influencing performance represent valuable advancements. However, the dataset limitations and lack of task-specific fine-tuning slightly temper the overall impact. Overall, the work is well-executed and provides useful guidance for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### How much are LLMs changing the language of academic papers after ChatGPT? A multi-database and full text analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09596v1)
- **Authors**: Kayvan Kousha, Mike Thelwall
- **Abstract**: This study investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) are influencing the language of academic papers by tracking 12 LLM-associated terms across six major scholarly databases (Scopus, Web of Science, PubMed, PubMed Central (PMC), Dimensions, and OpenAlex) from 2015 to 2024. Using over 2.4 million PMC open-access publications (2021-July 2025), we also analysed full texts to assess changes in the frequency and co-occurrence of these terms before and after ChatGPT's initial public release. Across databases, delve (+1,500%), underscore (+1,000%), and intricate (+700%) had the largest increases between 2022 and 2024. Growth in LLM-term usage was much higher in STEM fields than in social sciences and arts and humanities. In PMC full texts, the proportion of papers using underscore six or more times increased by over 10,000% from 2022 to 2025, followed by intricate (+5,400%) and meticulous (+2,800%). Nearly half of all 2024 PMC papers using any LLM term also included underscore, compared with only 3%-14% of papers before ChatGPT in 2022. Papers using one LLM term are now much more likely to include other terms. For example, in 2024, underscore strongly correlated with pivotal (0.449) and delve (0.311), compared with very weak associations in 2022 (0.032 and 0.018, respectively). These findings provide the first large-scale evidence based on full-text publications and multiple databases that some LLM-related terms are now being used much more frequently and together. The rapid uptake of LLMs to support scholarly publishing is a welcome development reducing the language barrier to academic publishing for non-English speakers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper "How much are LLMs changing the language of academic papers after ChatGPT? A multi-database and full-text analysis" investigates the impact of Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly ChatGPT, on academic writing. The authors tracked the frequency and co-occurrence of 12 LLM-associated terms across six major scholarly databases (Scopus, Web of Science, PubMed, PubMed Central (PMC), Dimensions, and OpenAlex) from 2015 to 2024.  Using a large dataset of PMC open-access publications, they analyzed full texts to assess changes before and after ChatGPT's public release. The study found a significant increase in the use of specific terms like "delve," "underscore," and "intricate," particularly in STEM fields.  The co-occurrence of LLM-associated terms also increased, suggesting a shift in academic writing styles influenced by LLMs. The study further explores disciplinary differences, the relationship between LLM-associated terms and retracted articles, and provides a comparative analysis of traditional research terms.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The paper offers a valuable contribution by presenting a large-scale, data-driven analysis of LLM influence on academic writing.  While some earlier studies have touched upon this topic, this paper expands significantly in terms of the dataset size (full-text analysis of over 2.4 million PMC publications), the number of databases analyzed, the time frame considered, and the range of analytical methods employed (frequency, co-occurrence, correlation). The full-text analysis and consideration of co-occurrence patterns are more novel than just counting single mentions, providing a deeper understanding. The comparative analysis of traditional and LLM-associated terms adds another layer of novelty.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a timely and important question regarding the evolving nature of academic discourse in the age of AI. The findings provide empirical evidence supporting the anecdotal observations about the increasing use of specific phrases and writing styles that might be influenced by LLMs. This is significant because it raises questions about the potential standardization and homogenization of academic writing, the potential impact on non-native English speakers, and the implications for academic integrity (given the association with retracted articles). While the paper does not definitively *prove* causation, it strongly suggests a correlation and provides a basis for further investigation. The exploration of disciplinary differences is also valuable, highlighting how the adoption of LLM-influenced language varies across fields. The observed increase in LLM term usage in retracted articles is concerning and warrants further study.

**Strengths:**

*   **Large-scale data analysis:** The study leverages a substantial dataset, enhancing the robustness and generalizability of the findings.
*   **Multiple databases:**  Analyzing multiple databases provides a more comprehensive view and reduces bias.
*   **Full-text analysis:**  Going beyond titles and abstracts to analyze full texts offers a more in-depth understanding.
*   **Comparative analysis:**  Comparing LLM-associated terms with traditional academic terms provides context.
*   **Clear research questions:** The research questions are well-defined and addressed systematically.
*   **Reproducible approach:** The use of publicly available data and methods enhances the reproducibility of the research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Causation vs. Correlation:** The paper primarily demonstrates correlations rather than proving causation. While the observed trends are suggestive, other factors could be contributing to the changes in language. It's possible some terms are becoming more common even outside LLMs.
*   **Term Selection:** The selection of LLM-associated terms is subjective. While the authors provide a rationale, other terms could have been included. There is not clear justification for the selection of these 12 terms over others.
*   **Limited Semantic Analysis:** The study focuses on the frequency and co-occurrence of terms without a deeper semantic analysis of how the meaning and usage of these terms might be evolving. The study admits that it has not investigated potential semantic or stylistic changes in sentence structure.
*   **Focus on PMC:** The in-depth full-text analysis is limited to PMC (open-access biomedical literature). It is unclear if the trends are generalizable to non-open access research or other subject areas.
*   **Over-reliance on specific databases:** Relying solely on Scopus may limit the study’s generalizability to the global research landscape, as the database has a bias towards journals published in the English language, as well as more reputable and prestigious journals

**Justification of Score:**

Despite its weaknesses, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing empirical evidence of the influence of LLMs on academic writing. The large-scale data analysis, exploration of co-occurrence patterns, and comparative analysis significantly enhance our understanding of the phenomenon. It raises important questions about academic integrity, standardization of language, and the impact on different disciplines and it is rigorous. While the study does not definitively prove causation, it provides a strong foundation for further research. The limitations regarding the selection of terms, lack of semantic analysis, and reliance on specific databases slightly reduce the impact of this study.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.DL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LAVA: Language Model Assisted Verbal Autopsy for Cause-of-Death Determination
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09602v1)
- **Authors**: Yiqun T. Chen, Tyler H. McCormick, Li Liu, Abhirup Datta
- **Abstract**: Verbal autopsy (VA) is a critical tool for estimating causes of death in resource-limited settings where medical certification is unavailable. This study presents LA-VA, a proof-of-concept pipeline that combines Large Language Models (LLMs) with traditional algorithmic approaches and embedding-based classification for improved cause-of-death prediction. Using the Population Health Metrics Research Consortium (PHMRC) dataset across three age categories (Adult: 7,580; Child: 1,960; Neonate: 2,438), we evaluate multiple approaches: GPT-5 predictions, LCVA baseline, text embeddings, and meta-learner ensembles. Our results demonstrate that GPT-5 achieves the highest individual performance with average test site accuracies of 48.6% (Adult), 50.5% (Child), and 53.5% (Neonate), outperforming traditional statistical machine learning baselines by 5-10%. Our findings suggest that simple off-the-shelf LLM-assisted approaches could substantially improve verbal autopsy accuracy, with important implications for global health surveillance in low-resource settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LAVA: Language Model Assisted Verbal Autopsy for Cause-of-Death Determination":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LA-VA (Language-model Assisted Verbal Autopsy), a pipeline that combines large language models (LLMs) with traditional machine learning methods for improving the accuracy of cause-of-death (COD) determination from verbal autopsy (VA) data. The authors evaluate the pipeline on the PHMRC dataset across three age categories, comparing GPT-5 predictions, LCVA baselines, text embeddings, and meta-learner ensembles. Their results show that GPT-5 achieves the highest individual performance, outperforming traditional methods. The study suggests that LLM-assisted approaches can significantly improve VA accuracy, which is important for global health surveillance in resource-limited settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the application of recent advancements in large language models (specifically GPT-5) to the problem of cause-of-death determination from verbal autopsy data. While previous studies have explored using machine learning on VA data, this paper demonstrates the potential of LLMs to capture nuanced information from narratives and improve prediction accuracy. The systematic comparison with existing methods (LCVA, embeddings) and the use of a meta-learner ensemble are also valuable.  The use of prompt engineering to encode contextual information related to geographical region also offers a novel approach to dealing with distribution shift.

*   **Significance:** Verbal autopsy remains a critical tool in areas lacking medical certification. Improved VA accuracy directly translates to better public health planning, resource allocation, and targeted interventions. The paper's findings highlight the potential for LLMs to scale up VA analysis in low-resource settings. The study also provides valuable insights into the strengths and limitations of different approaches (LLMs vs. embeddings) and emphasizes the importance of calibration for population-level estimations.  The ability to make inferences regarding cause of death for both children and neonates is particularly impactful.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive Evaluation: The paper conducts a thorough evaluation on a large, well-established dataset (PHMRC) with a cross-site design, which strengthens the generalizability of the findings.
    *   Comparison with Baselines: The comparison with traditional statistical and machine learning methods provides a clear benchmark and highlights the improvements achieved by LA-VA.
    *   Focus on Real-World Challenges: The paper addresses key challenges in VA analysis, such as domain shift and the need for population-level accuracy.
    *   Detailed Methodology: The authors provide a detailed description of the LA-VA pipeline, including the LLM prompts, embedding techniques, and ensemble methods, which promotes reproducibility.
    *   Emphasis on Population-Level Calibration: The explicit focus on calibration is vital for ensuring that the model is useful for informing decision-making.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on GPT-5: The reliance on a proprietary model (GPT-5) limits the accessibility and reproducibility of the study. While GPT-5 represents a current state-of-the-art LLM, future work should explore open-source LLMs.
    *   Limited Exploration of Fine-Tuning:  The study uses a zero-shot approach with GPT-5. While the prompt engineering provides a valuable baseline, fine-tuning GPT-5 or another LLM on VA data may yield even better performance.
    *   Historical Data: The study uses data from 2007-2010. While the data is well-established and representative of VA datasets, the current VA approaches and practices may have changed.
    *   Multilingual Validation: The validation is primarily English-centric, and VA is practiced worldwide. The authors mention this in their conclusion, and state the need for multimodal VA datasets such as COMSA and CHAMPS.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of automated VA systems and improve global health surveillance. The study provides a strong case for using LLMs in VA analysis and can inspire future research on fine-tuning LLMs, integrating other data sources, and addressing ethical considerations.

*   **Justification for Score:** Given the novelty of applying LLMs to VA, the comprehensive evaluation, and the potential impact on global health, this paper represents a significant contribution. However, some limitations temper the score. The reliance on GPT-5 reduces accessibility, and the lack of fine-tuning suggests room for improvement. I give it a score of **7**. The use of prompt engineering offers an improvement over existing methods, and its use in neonates and children in addition to adults increases its significance, but the heavy reliance on a propriety technology slightly detracts from its potential.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### Mechanistic Learning with Guided Diffusion Models to Predict Spatio-Temporal Brain Tumor Growth
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09610v1)
- **Authors**: Daria Laslo, Efthymios Georgiou, Marius George Linguraru, Andreas Rauschecker, Sabine Muller, Catherine R. Jutzeler, Sarah Bruningk
- **Abstract**: Predicting the spatio-temporal progression of brain tumors is essential for guiding clinical decisions in neuro-oncology. We propose a hybrid mechanistic learning framework that combines a mathematical tumor growth model with a guided denoising diffusion implicit model (DDIM) to synthesize anatomically feasible future MRIs from preceding scans. The mechanistic model, formulated as a system of ordinary differential equations, captures temporal tumor dynamics including radiotherapy effects and estimates future tumor burden. These estimates condition a gradient-guided DDIM, enabling image synthesis that aligns with both predicted growth and patient anatomy. We train our model on the BraTS adult and pediatric glioma datasets and evaluate on 60 axial slices of in-house longitudinal pediatric diffuse midline glioma (DMG) cases. Our framework generates realistic follow-up scans based on spatial similarity metrics. It also introduces tumor growth probability maps, which capture both clinically relevant extent and directionality of tumor growth as shown by 95th percentile Hausdorff Distance. The method enables biologically informed image generation in data-limited scenarios, offering generative-space-time predictions that account for mechanistic priors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a mechanistic learning framework for predicting spatio-temporal brain tumor growth. It combines a mathematical model (ODE) of tumor dynamics, which incorporates radiotherapy effects, with a guided denoising diffusion implicit model (DDIM) to generate anatomically realistic future MRI scans.  The mechanistic model provides estimates of future tumor burden, which then guides the DDIM to synthesize plausible images.  The model is trained on BraTS datasets and evaluated on longitudinal pediatric diffuse midline glioma (DMG) cases.  The framework produces realistic follow-up scans based on spatial similarity metrics and generates tumor growth probability maps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the *integration* of a mechanistic tumor growth model with a generative diffusion model for longitudinal brain tumor prediction.  While both mechanistic modeling and diffusion models are established techniques, their combination in this context, particularly with the guidance mechanism, is novel. Prior work using generative models for brain tumor imaging exists, but spatio-temporal progression, especially in data-scarce scenarios, is less explored. Leveraging mechanistic priors to constrain the generative model addresses a crucial challenge in medical image synthesis: ensuring biological plausibility, especially when dealing with limited longitudinal data.
*   **Significance:**  Predicting tumor growth is crucial for clinical decision-making in neuro-oncology. This work attempts to provide a tool to generate realistic future scans, potentially aiding in treatment planning, predicting symptom progression, and facilitating anticipatory management.  The ability to generate biologically informed images in data-limited scenarios (like rare and aggressive DMGs) is highly valuable. The tumor growth probability maps could be useful for clinicians to assess treatment effects and potential tumor spread.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a Critical Need:** Focuses on a clinically relevant problem: predicting tumor growth in neuro-oncology.
    *   **Combines Strengths of Different Approaches:** Combines the explanatory power of mechanistic models with the image generation capabilities of diffusion models.
    *   **Handles Data Scarcity:** Addresses the challenge of limited longitudinal data, which is a common issue in rare diseases.
    *   **Generates Biologically Plausible Images:** The guidance mechanism ensures that the generated images are not just visually realistic but also consistent with known tumor growth patterns.
    *   **Quantifiable Results:** Provides both qualitative (visual assessment) and quantitative (SSIM, Hausdorff Distance) evaluation of the framework's performance.
    *   **Includes tumor growth probability maps:** The probability maps may be valuable for clinicians.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Evaluation Dataset:** The evaluation is primarily based on a relatively small dataset of 60 slices from 8 DMG patients.  A larger and more diverse dataset would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** The performance of the framework is sensitive to the choice of parameters like noise level (NL) and regressor scale (SR(ct)). The grid search helps, but optimizing these parameters for different patient populations or tumor types could be challenging.
    *   **Simplified Mechanistic Model:** The mechanistic model, while capturing key aspects, is still a simplification of complex biological processes. There is a trade-off between model complexity and interpretability.
    *   **Dependency on Manual Segmentation:** The framework relies on manual tumor segmentations for training and evaluation, which are time-consuming and subject to inter-rater variability.
    *   **Generalization:** The method relies on having a minimal number of imaging time points. This makes it difficult to use when very little data is available.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Generative models, especially diffusion models, can be computationally expensive to train and sample from.
*   **Potential Impact:** The paper presents a promising approach for spatio-temporal tumor growth prediction.  If further validated and refined, it could become a valuable tool for clinical decision-making in neuro-oncology.
*   **Justification for Score:** The paper demonstrates a novel and potentially impactful approach, particularly in the context of data-scarce and clinically challenging scenarios. However, the limited evaluation dataset, parameter sensitivity, and dependence on manual segmentations prevent a higher score. The simplified model and the need for minimal imaging time points for the model make it difficult to apply more broadly. The novelty of approach somewhat outweighs the limitations of the evaluation, resulting in a middling score.
